# Membrane-free CO2 hydrogenation electrolyzer for salt precipitation management in acidic electrochemical CO2 reduction

- Document type: peer_review
- Journal: Nature Communications
- DOI: https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-68600-3
- Published: 2026-01-17
- Article ID: s41467-026-68600-3

Peer Review File
Membrane-free CO2 hydrogenation electrolyzer for salt
precipitation management in acidic electrochemical CO2
reduction
Corresponding Author: Professor Wei Chen
This file contains all reviewer reports in order by version, followed by all author rebuttals in order by version.
 
Version 0: 
Reviewer comments: 
Reviewer #1 
(Remarks to the Author) 
The manuscript titled "Membrane-free CO2 hydrogenation electrolyzer for salt precipitation management in acidic
electrochemical CO2 reduction" addresses a practical limitation in acidic CO2 electroreduction (ECR) systems — salt
precipitation due to proton transport inefficiency. By proposing a membrane-free electrolyzer coupled with hydrogen
oxidation reaction (HOR), the authors aim to stabilize catholyte pH, suppress carbonate formation, and improve system
stability. The study includes thoughtful electrochemical evaluations, a techno-economic analysis (TEA), and mechanistic
insights into ion transport. While, the novelty is limited, particularly regarding the electrolyzer configuration. The proposed
strategies echo existing literature without providing sufficient mechanistic differentiation or rigorous theoretical validation.
The role of ηH+ is discussed as a central concept, yet it remains empirically defined without being quantitatively supported
by theoretical models. Therefore, this manuscript is not suitable for publication in Nature Communications currently. 
1. The membrane-free + HOR configuration has been explored previously (e.g., Joule, Volume 6, Issue 12, 2745-2761). The
authors need to clearly explain the difference between their work and previous ones. 
2. The definition of ηH+ is operational and lacks a rigorous basis. No modeling (e.g., transport simulations) is presented to
support how membrane properties or electrolyte composition influence proton transfer. 
3. The authors neglect possible effects of hydrogen concentration variation, gas crossover, or local pH shifts at the anode
during long-term operation. 
4. Is the Bi oxidation state truly preserved under acidic bias? 
5. The rationale for using Bi0.5Ag0.5 is mainly based on conductivity enhancement, which is not novel. The role of Bi-Ag
interaction in intermediate stabilization or CO2 binding needs to be better elaborated. 
6. Supplying pure H2 to the anode may not be economically viable for large-scale systems. Authors should at least discuss
alternative hydrogen sources (e.g., internal HER coupling) or system integration strategies. 
7. To highlight the advantage of the membrane-free acidic system, it would be informative to include direct performance
comparisons (FE, energy efficiency, voltage) with neutral or alkaline membrane-based ECR systems using the same Bi–Ag
catalyst. 
8. Several key characterizations, including catalyst structure, long-term stability data, and electrolyte compositions, are
buried in the supplementary section. Some of these should be promoted to the main text to ensure scientific transparency. 
Reviewer #2 
(Remarks to the Author) 
This manuscript reported a membrane-free reactor for CO2 reduction in an acidic electrolyte. Compared with membrane-
based electrolyzers, the membrane-free design maintains a stable electrolyte pH, which is crucial for acidic CO2 reduction.
To address challenges associated with this design, they utilized electrochemical hydrogen oxidation to replace the oxygen
evolution reaction, thereby suppressing formic acid overoxidation at the anode. Additionally, they proposed coupling
methanol oxidation with the hydrogen evolution reaction to reduce H2 costs. As a result, the reactor exhibited high
selectivity, single-pass efficiency, and stability. More impressively, they emphasized the often-overlooked significance of
proton transfer efficiency across the membrane, which greatly affects the pH of the catholyte. This is important for accurately
evaluate acidic CO2 reduction performance and maintain long-term stability. Therefore, this manuscript could be accepted

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 2 -->

for publication in Nature Communications after addressing the following questions. 
1. The authors used the same pH solution as both the anolyte and catholyte. However, some acidic CO2 reduction studies
use a pure acid solution as the anolyte. What is the influence of the anolyte composition? 
2. The authors developed BiAg catalyst for CO2-to-formic acid conversion. How does its performance compare with
previously reported catalysts? Additionally, how does it’s performance in neutral and alkaline electrolytes? 
3. In the membrane-based electrolyzer design, why does the catholyte pH stabilize at ~7-8 after several hours of testing? 
4. The authors reported a 50-hour acidic stability. How does this result compare with current literature values? 
5. The TEA analysis did not clearly explain the calculation of the liquid product separation cost, which needs to be refined
for greater clarity. Furthermore, the liquid product separation cost should vary among different electrolyzers due to
differences in catholyte usage. The HER//AEM//MOR system should also account for the separation cost. The transformation
cost from formate to formic acid should also be considered in the HER//AEM//MOR system. 
6. Some statements in the SI are inaccurate, such as electrocatalytic ECR activity. Please carefully check the statements. 
Reviewer #3 
(Remarks to the Author) 
In this study, the authors propose a membrane-free electrolyzer to enhance the stability and economic viability of
electrocatalytic CO2 reduction in acidic systems. Experimental results demonstrate that the membrane-free electrolyzer
significantly improves operational stability from 8 hours to 50 hours. To further optimize the system's economic efficiency, the
authors employ hydrogen oxidation reaction (HOR) to replace the conventional oxygen evolution reaction (OER) at the
anode and introduce a tandem reaction strategy (HOR-CEM-MOR) to reduce overall operational costs. Upon careful
evaluation, the reviewer considers the current study presents critical shortcomings in practical applicability and data
accuracy that undermine the validity of its conclusions. Specific concerns are outlined below: 
1. The experimental results report a dilute formic acid concentration of 0.1 M was produced in CO2 reduction system.
However, the techno-economic analysis (TEA) lacks rigor, as the authors fail to account for the energy and costs required to
concentrate and purify the product to ≥95% purity, which is essential for accurate economic evaluation. 
2. To fairly highlight the advantages of the membrane-free strategy, comparative analyses under equivalent conditions are
imperative. Notably, the parameters for the CRR//CEM//OER process were derived from a 10-mm flow channel plate,
whereas those for CRR//HOR and CRR/CEM/MOR were based on a 2-mm plate. This discrepancy invalidates direct
economic comparisons. 
3. As the authors emphasize that acidic systems avoid the acidification and separation costs associated with alkaline-
derived formate. However, the CRR//CEM//MOR configuration employs an alkaline electrolyte, this introduces additional
formic acid purification expenses, which are not considered in the current TEA. 
4. Carbonate formation typically limits CO2 reduction stability to <200 h due to pore-blocking effects in CO2 transport
channels. Despite adopting a membrane-free system, the reported stability (50 h) remains insufficient to demonstrate
practical superiority. Furthermore, this limited stability raises concerns about operational feasibility in real-world applications.
5. The reviewer questions whether reactant crossover from the anode to the cathode via the gas diffusion layer (GDL) in the
membrane-free system would increase gas separation costs, a factor omitted in the analysis. 
6. The manuscript lacks a comprehensive list of parameters (e.g., energy inputs, material costs, separation efficiencies) used
in the TEA, hindering reproducibility and critical assessment. 
7. The electrocatalytic reaction area is not explicitly stated. Detailed experimental information, including electrode
dimensions and active surface area, must be provided. 
8. The currency unit "dollars" should be standardized as "USD" throughout the manuscript for clarity and consistency. 
Reviewer #4 
(Remarks to the Author) 
This article by Da et al. showed a detailed study on using a membrane-free electrolyzer design to manage pH at the cathode
and hence mitigate salt precipitation during CO2 electrolysis, which is a major challenge in the field. They systematically
compared the effect of pH stabilization with different electrode pairing strategies (ECR/CEM/OER, ECR/CEM/HOR,
ECR/OER, and ECR/HOR), identifying the membraneless ECR/HOR as the most promising configuration in terms of formic
acid faradaic efficiency, proton transfer efficiency, pH management, and cell voltage, etc. They also demonstrated a cascade
of ECR/HOR and ECR/AEM/MOR electrolyzers that show promise for practical application based on the TEA. Although the
concept of membrane-free CER/HOR electrolyzers is not new (see Fu et al., Science 379, 707–712 (2023); Pang et al. Joule
6, 12, 2745 - 2761; and Lazouski et al. Nat Catal 3, 463–469 (2020), none of which were cited), the authors added value by
comparing the different cell configurations and extending to a tandem scheme while achieving appreciable performance in
terms of current density, Faradaic efficiency, and single pass conversion efficiency. The study presented here should be of
interest to the electrochemistry and the broader CO2 utilization communities. However, the following concerns need to be
addressed before the article is suitable for publication. 
1. The TEA looks promising and can be helpful for projecting the competitive advantage of the new technology, but I have
some questions and concerns. (i) A more detailed written description of the assumptions for “the liquid product separation” is
required. Currently, the method(s) of separation are not described, key assumptions about the concentration of the solutions
entering and leaving the separation unit are absent, and there are no citations for the Capex and operating costs. 
For the
tandem process involving formate production from MOR, was the liquid separation cost for the MOR-derived formate
accounted for as well? 
Salty, alkaline formate/formic acid will have very little value. (ii) The TEA should be carried out as a
discounted cash flow analysis using a typical discount rate (e.g. 10%). It seems that this was not done. (iii) Was the

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 3 -->

electrolyzer Capex of $919/m^2, derived from a value of $250 $/kW (which is very low), applied to both the water electrolyzer
and the CO2 electrolyzer? This should be clearly stated. It’s also not clear if the 250 $/kW is for a PEM or liquid alkaline
electrolyzer. (iv) It would be very helpful to briefly state some of the key assumptions for the TEA in the main article (e.g., cost
of electrolyzer, Capex, method of separation, price of CO2, price of MeOH) so that the reader can better understand the
baseline scenario being presented. 
Optional: a sensitivity analysis would be helpful. 
2. Regarding Figure 3, although the ECR/HOR configuration and the 2 mm gap significantly lowered the cell voltage, 1.7 V
is still a relatively high cell voltage, especially if ECR (-0.2 V vs. SHE) and HOR 
（
0 V vs. SHE) are paired. Can the authors
break down the overpotential losses? Also, with the ECR/OER and ECR/CEM/OER configurations, the cell voltages are
between 5 and 6 V. Did the authors observe any degradation of the carbon paper substrate as carbon is prone to oxidation?
For the ECR/HOR configuration without a membrane, why is the cell voltage so high (~6 V)? What is the origin of the
resistance? 
3. How scalable is the membraneless electrolyzer design? It seems that all results for the CO2 electrolyzer were obtained
using very small (1 cm^2) electrodes. Did the authors attempt any measurements at a larger scale to see how/if performance
changes? 
4. How stable can the electrolyzer system be beyond 50 hours, which is a relatively short time for a durability test for
electrolyzer technology? 
5. Since CO was observed as shown in Figure 2, did the authors observe any CO poisoning effect with the Pt anode? If so,
how significant is it, especially during long-term operation? 
6. Regarding Supplementary Figure 21, the bicarbonate peak should be overlaid with and compared to the other
configurations as shown in Supplementary Figure 19, especially since the y-axis does not indicate the scale. 
Minor comments: 
The authors should also list all the assumptions made during the simulation of the scenarios as shown in Figure 1 b-c. 
The authors should define MOR (methanol oxidation reaction) when they first introduced it.

Version 1: 
Reviewer comments: 
Reviewer #1 
(Remarks to the Author) 
I have carefully reviewed the revised manuscript and the authors’ point-by-point responses to my initial comments. The
authors have provided additional experiments, analyses, and discussions that partially address my concerns, including
performance comparisons with membrane-based systems, qualitative discussion of ηH+ transport, and mechanistic insights
into the Bi–Ag catalyst. These revisions improve the clarity and completeness of the work. However, several of my original
concerns remain only partially resolved. 
1. While the Nernst–Planck equation is now introduced, no quantitative modeling or parameter measurement is provided.
Please consider simplified transport simulations (e.g., COMSOL) to quantify the effects of membrane thickness, ion
selectivity, and electrolyte composition on ηH+. 
2. The SHINERS data suggest a lower onset potential for *CO2- formation on Bi0.5–Ag0.5, but the mechanistic explanation
remains focused on conductivity. Please elaborate on whether Ag modifies the adsorption energies of *CO2- or *OCHO, or
alters the electronic structure of Bi. 
3. The proposed MOR–HER coupling is presented as a proof-of-concept, but lacks quantitative energy and cost analysis.
Please compare the energy efficiency of external H2 supply versus internal H
₂
 generation (including MOR energy demand
and CO2 byproduct management), and provide a preliminary TEA for industrial-scale operation. 
Reviewer #2 
(Remarks to the Author) 
The authors have supplemented related experimental results, and have well addressed most of my comments. I recommend
the publication of this manuscript.

Reviewer #3 
(Remarks to the Author) 
By reviewing the point-by-point response to the reviewers, the reviewer believes that the authors' citation and methodology
regarding TEA still have significant flaws. For example, in the response to comment 1, Reference 7 does not involve the

32870-32879 (2024).) 
Similarly, in Comment 3, I could not find the corresponding description—Reference 21 does not include an author named

）
 
Therefore, regarding these two issues about TEA, I believe the authors have not addressed my concerns because I cannot
assess the accuracy of their response. The data sources provided by the authors are unreliable, so I do not recommend
accepting this article for publication in this journal. 
Finally, I noticed that on page 12 of the manuscript, it mentions that the formic acid production concentration is 0.1 M. Thus,

Reviewer #4 
(Remarks to the Author) 
In the updated TEA calculations, I believe there is a significant error in the calculation of the Capex for the CO2 electrolyzer.
Specifically, on page 50 of the response document where the authors shown their calculation of the area-normalized Capex
based on the reported Capex of traditional PEM water electrolyzers (~ $560 USD/kW), they assume a current density of 0.17
A/cm2. This is incorrect. 
Conventional PEM electrolyzers should have a reference current density of closer to 1.7 A/cm2, not
0.17 A/cm2. As a result, the area-based Capex should be $16,184 USD/m^2, NOT $1,618 USD/m^2. 
As an aside, the
authors should be careful with their dimensional analysis and more clearly spell out the conversion from kW to W and cm^2
to m^2. 
Supplementary Fig. 39 (sensitivity analysis)- I find it confusing to interpret this figure. I suggest that the authors re-draw it in
the form of a more traditional “tornado plot” where the baseline, “high”, and “low” values of the dependent variable are all
plotted on the same axis instead of two identical axes as was done here. Furthermore, I suggest the authors plot the
levelized cost of FA production as the dependent variable being plotted rather than NPV. 
NPV is not very useful on its own
since it is so dependent on plant size. 
Regarding scalability, it is encouraging that the new larger cell also demonstrated high FE towards FA, but the authors
should also add overlaid polarization (current density vs. voltage) curves for both the 0.5 cm × 2 cm and 2 cm × 2 cm cells.
This will be very helpful to see how the performance is affected by the increased electrode size. 
Regarding CO poisoning of the Pt anode catalyst: 
I do not agree with the authors' interpretation of their new results.
Supplementary Fig. 28 clearly shows a CO stripping peak around +0.8 V vs. RHE; below this potential it is likely that Pt has
a difficult time doing HOR (as is well known in H2 fuel cell literature). 
That means that the anode probably needs at least
+0.8 V of extra overpotential to oxidize CO and be active towards HOR. 
i.e., at high enough voltage, the poison will be
removed, but device efficiency suffers from the higher voltage.

Reviewer #3 
(Remarks to the Author)

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 5 -->

I have carefully reviewed the authors' detailed content regarding the TEA results and appreciate their contributions in this
area, which have enhanced the rigor of the scientific research. However, I believe the authors have not fully addressed the
issues I raised. My primary concern lies in the cost consumption during the concentration process of the dilute formic acid
stream obtained in this experiment. In their response to Comment 2, the authors cited content from Reference 9 and
Reference 12 to support their analysis of the concentration cost of the formic acid product. However, the reviewer noted that
in Reference 9, the original authors used a formic acid feed concentration of 10%wt, rather than the concentration of 0.1
mol/L ≈ 0.46%wt obtained in the authors' article. Therefore, the reviewer considers it inappropriate to directly apply the
energy consumption data from this concentration process. The current data can not support the results presented in the
article. 
Reviewer #4 
(Remarks to the Author) 
1. Regarding the Capex of the CO2 electrolyzer, I still maintain that the authors are making a major mistake that results in
significantly underestimating the value of the electrolyzer Capex. I see that they are using the same input numbers from a
previous paper (Energy & Fuels, 2023, 37, 23, 17997–18008, Supplementary Information Page 12, Fig. R3), but when I
looked deeper into this, it seems that the assumption of a reference current density of 0.17 A/cm2 in the Energy & Fuels
paper is incorrect. It’s also worth pointing out that their Eqn. (6) is missing a term for the total cell area, another sloppy
mistake. 
From page 4 of the cited 2019 NREL Report (“Manufacturing Cost Analysis for Proton Exchange Membrane Water
Electrolyzers”, an excellent resource), one can clearly see the reference voltage of 1.7 V, and the current density should be
1.7 A/cm2, not 0.17 A/cm2. 
A current density of 1.7 A/cm2 is much more typical of a PEM water electrolyzer, which is the
basis of the NREL study. From all previous electrolyzer TEA studies by others or that I have done myself, the electrolyzer
Capex is based on normalization of the total electrolyzer system Capex by the rated power (i.e. Area x reference operating
current density x reference voltage), not an arbitrarily low current density like 0.17 A/cm2. Why would a reference current
density of 0.17 A/cm2 be chosen when the PEM electrolyzer typically operates 1.5-2.0 A/cm2? 
Thus, it seems evident that
there is a mistake in the Energy & Fuels paper (and hence, the current manuscript) that leads to the CO2 electrolyzer Capex
(expressed in $/m^2) being 1 order of magnitude lower than it should be. 
In the pdf version of my comments, I've included the reference table from the 2019 NREL report cited in the Energy & Fuels
paper that clearly shows a reference current density of 1.7 A/cm2, not 0.17 A/cm2. 
2. Regarding CO poisoning: my original point in my previous comment was that the CO2 electrolyzer is operating at such
high voltages (4-5 V) that it is not surprising that there is no CO oxidation signal seen during CO stripping voltammetry
measurement recorded at the end of the durability test. 
Under these conditions, the anode is likely polarized to such high
values that any CO that crosses over from the cathode is easily oxidized and therefore doesn’t show up in the subsequent
CO stripping voltammetry experiments. 
It may be true that CO poisoning isn’t occurring at these conditions, but that’s only
because a very high cell voltage (and thus lower conversion efficiency) was used. The lack of a CO stripping peak after the
stability test doesn’t 
prove that CO is not crossing over, and it doesn’t mean that CO poisoning is not an issue. 
I would guess
that the use of a more CO tolerant anode catalyst like PtRu would lead to significantly reduced cell voltage. 
Thus, I still
disagree with the authors’ statement that there is no CO crossover in their cell in this line: “ These results demonstrate that
the CO generated at the cathode did not crossover to the anode to cause CO poisoning under the tested flow cell
conditions.” 
At the least, I feel like this statement is very misleading and should be changed.

Version 3: 
Reviewer comments: 
Reviewer #3 
(Remarks to the Author) 
The economic assessment of formic acid concentration in the current TEA is unreasonable and lacks detailed, solid
information to support the accuracy of the adopted methodology. 
The systems documented in the literature typically involve the separation of HCOONa solutions. In contrast, this study deals
with a mixture of K2SO4 and HCOOH. These are entirely different reaction systems. Consequently, the energy consumption
values reported in the literature cannot be directly applied to this specific mixture. 
It is currently unclear to the reader how the electrodialysis method is implemented to concentrate the K2SO4/HCOOH
mixture, particularly given the complication of anion crossover. Therefore, the reviewer finds the current calculation method
to be unfounded. 
The approach lacks support from both relevant literature and any provided experimental or simulation data. As a result, it is
my opinion that the current methodology cannot reliably support the presented overall economic calculations.

REVIEWER COMMENTS 
 
Reviewer #1  
The manuscript titled "Membrane -free CO 2 hydrogenation electrolyzer for salt 
precipitation management in acidic electrochemical CO2 reduction" addresses a 
practical limitation in acidic CO2 electroreduction (ECR) systems — salt precipitation 
due to proton transport inefficiency. By proposing a membrane -free electrolyzer 
coupled with hydrogen oxidation reaction (HOR), the authors aim to stabilize catholyte 
pH, suppress carbonate formation, and improve system stability. The study includes 
thoughtful electrochemical evaluations, a techno -economic analysis (TEA), and 
mechanistic insights into ion transport. While, the novelty is limited, p articularly 
regarding the electrolyzer configuration. The proposed strategies echo existing 
literature without providing sufficient mechanistic differentiation or rigorous 
theoretical validation. The role of ηH+ is discussed as a central concept, yet it remains 
empirically defined without being quantitatively supported by theoretical models. 
Therefore, this manuscript is not suitable for publication in Nature Communications 
currently. 
 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for the constructive and detailed comments. Previous 
pioneering works on membrane -free electrolyzers coupled with hydrogen oxidation 
have been applied to CO 2/bicarbonate reduction, and we have cited and highlighted 
these works in the revised manuscript (ref. 21). While the overall design may appear 
similar, our study addresses different challenges. Specifically, we focus on catholyte 
pH fluctuations in acidic CO 2 reduction, which contrasts with the previously reported 
systems operating in alkaline or neutral environments. As a result , our work 
demonstrated sustained pH stabilization and effective suppression of salt precipitation 
over 208 hours at a current density of 100 mA cm−2, an achievement rarely reported for 
membrane-free systems. Additionally, we now include a qualitative discussion on how 
membrane properties and electrolyte composition influence 𝜂𝐻+ . Please check the 
response to Comments 1 and 2 for details. A point-by-point response is provided below. 
  
1. The membrane-free + HOR configuration has been explored previously (e.g., Joule, 
Volume 6, Issue 12, 2745 -2761). The authors need to clearly explain the difference 
between their work and previous ones. 
 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for the thoughtful comment and for highlighting the prior work 
published in Joule (2022, 6, 12, 2745 -2761), which reported a membrane -free

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 8 -->

P
A
G
E
2
68 
 
bicarbonate electrolyzer utilizing porous flow -through electrodes to facilitate pH 
balancing and in situ CO 2 generation upstream of the cathode. Although both works 
utilize membrane-free electrolyzers and coupl e HOR at the anode , our work is 
fundamentally distinct in its design principles, operating conditions, and performance 
objectives, as summarized below: 
 
a) Electrolyte and CO2 source 
The previous work utilized an aqueous bicarbonate electrolyte, where CO2 is generated 
in situ by the reaction between H + (from anodic HOR) and HCO 3−. This approach 
enables efficient pH rebalancing between adjacent electrodes in a sequential flow 
design. In contrast, our system directly feeds gaseous CO2 into the cathode 
compartment while maintaining an acidic electrolyte environment (pH ≈ 1.8), enabling 
stable operation without carbonate formation or precipitation. Furthermore, the use of 
a direct CO2 gas feed in our system offers a more straightforward and controllable way 
to regulate CO 2 availability at the cathode, independent of local pH or proton flux. 
While in situ CO 2 generation from bicarbonate can be advantageous for integrated 
carbon capture scenarios, it may introduce additional variability due to its dependence 
on acid−base reaction kinetics. In contrast, our approach decouples CO 2 supply from 
electrolyte composition, allowing for more consistent evaluation of electrochemical 
performance, particularly under industrially relevant conditions. 
 
b) pH balancing strategy 
The previous work achieved pH regulation through a sequential flow -through design 
between electrodes . In contrast, we employed a chemically self -balanced system 
wherein protons generated from HOR at the anode neutralize the hydroxide ions 
generated from ECR at the cathode. This intrinsic acid−base coupling maintains a stable 
acidic electrolyte environment throughout operation and avoids carbonate 
precipitation—one of the major degradation pathways in aqueous CO2 electrolysis. 
 
c) Target product and selectivity challenge 
The previous work focused on CO production under near -neutral conditions, whereas 
our work targets HCOOH generation in acidic media. Our system demonstrates high 
HCOOH faradaic efficiency (~90%), suppressed HER, low cell voltage  (1.7 V), and 
elevated single-pass CO2 utilization (77%). 
 
d) Operational current densities and practical relevance 
The previous work was evaluated at low current densities (<50 mA cm−2), suitable for 
fundamental investigation. O ur electrolyzer achieves stable operation at industrial 
relevant current densities of 100−400 mA cm−2. This high-rate performance, together

with the membrane -free design and low cell voltage, significantly enhances the 
industrial application of our system. 
 
In summary, although both studies explored membrane-free electrolyzers coupled with 
hydrogen oxidation at the anode, their objectives, targeted challenges, reactor designs, 
and reactants and products were fundamentally different. We have cited and highlighted 
the pioneering work on Page 9, also copied below: 
 
Main text, Page 9 
Inspired by pioneering work 21 on bicarbonate reduction for CO production using 
membrane-free electrolyzers coupled with HOR in the anode, we coupled HOR with 
ECR in a membrane -free CO 2 hydrogenation electrolyzer (ECR//HOR, Fig. 3c) to 
address the issue of HCOOH oxidation at the anode 51 caused by the absence of a 
membrane (Supplementary Figs. 15-17). 
 
2. The definition of ηH+ is operational and lacks a rigorous basis. No modeling (e.g., 
transport simulations) is presented to support how membrane properties or electrolyte 
composition influence proton transfer. 
 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful comment. The parameter 𝜂𝐻+ is defined as an 
operational, charge -normalized descriptor to quantify proton transfer relative to 
electron passage during CO 2 electroreduction. This simplified metric provides an 
intuitive connection between measurable electrochemical para meters (current, time, 
and electrolyte pH) and the influence of proton transport on catholyte stability. While 
𝜂𝐻+  is directly influenced by  transport in microcosmic , it provides a practical 
framework for assessing the membrane ’s ability to maintain catholyte acidity and 
suppress carbonate formation under operating conditions. 
 
We agreed with the reviewer’s opinion that the  𝜂𝐻+ directly influenced by transport in 
microcosmic. To this end, we have introduced the Nernst–Planck equation (Eq. 2) to 
qualitatively illustrate the contributions of diffusion and electric field-driven migration 
to the total ionic flux across the membrane. While we have not performed full -scale 
transport simulations in this study, the Nernst–Planck framework lays the foundation 
for future modeling efforts that can quantitatively assess the role of membrane 
chemistry, thickness, ion selectivity, and electrolyte composition on proton transport 
efficiency. We have included additional qualitative discussion based on the Nernst -
Planck equation to better describe the influence of membrane property and electrolyte 
composition to 𝜂𝐻+. As shown in Page 6, also copied below.

Main text, Page 6 
The overall flux of arbitrary ions i through a membrane, 𝐽𝑖 , consists of three terms: the 
diffusion flux ( Ji, diffusion ), caused by the chemical potential gradient; the electric 
transference (Ji, electric), caused by the electric potential gradient; and the convection flux 
Ji, convection, associated with the bulk  velocity41,42. The overall flux can be expressed by 
the following equation (2): 
𝐽𝑖 =  𝐽𝑖,𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝑢𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 +  𝐽𝑖,𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑐 +  𝐽𝑖,𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 =  −𝐷𝑖 (∇𝐶𝑖 +  𝑧𝑖𝐶𝑖
𝐹
𝑅𝑇 ∇𝜑) + 𝐶𝑖𝑣        (2) 
where Di, Ci, zi are the diffusion coefficient, concentration, and valence of the arbitrary 
ion i, respectively. F is the Faraday constant, R is the gas constant, T is temperature, 𝜑 
is the electrical potential, and 𝑣 is bulk fluid velocity. For homogenous and nonporous 
membranes, the convection term can be neglected. This relation is known as the Nernst-
Planck equation and applies to all mobile ions present 34. In an acidic ECR system, 
several factors influence the overall Ji. Firstly, the properties of the membrane, such as 
its chemical structure, water content, pore size, will affect the Di and thus affect the Ji, 
diffusion. Membranes with higher Di facilitate H + transport and help minimize the pH 
gradient between the anode and cathode. Secondly, the composition of the anolyte and 
catholyte, including the anolyte species (with or without alkaline cations), 
concentration, as well as the concentration gradient between the anolyte and catholyte, 
also influence Ji, diffusion. For example, the transport of competing cations can hinder H+ 
transport, while a high H + concentration at the anode enhances H + transport due to a 
steeper concentration gradient. Thirdly, the applied potentials affect the Ji, electric . A 
higher voltage between the anode and cathode can enhance H+ transport from the anode 
to the cathode. Consequently, the overall Ji will directly influence the pH changes of 
the catholyte and anolyte, and finally influence the acidic ECR performance.  
 
 
3. The authors neglect possible effects of hydrogen concentration variation, gas 
crossover, or local pH shifts at the anode during long-term operation. 
 
Response: 
 
We appreciate the reviewer ’s insightful comment regarding the possible influence of 
hydrogen concentration variation, gas crossover, and local pH shifts at the anode during 
long-term operation. These factors are indeed important in practical electrolyzer 
operation. To address this concern and exclude their possible influence, we conducted 
a series of control experiments:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 11 -->

P
A
G
E
5
68 
 
a) Hydrogen concentration variation 
We would like to clarify that pure H2 was used as the anode gas feed in our electrolysis 
experiments, thereby excluding the variable of hydrogen dilution due to mixed gas 
compositions. To further evaluate the influence of hydrogen concentration on anodic 
performance, we conducted HOR measurements using a three -electrode ECR//HOR 
electrolyzer under different H 2/Ar gas mixtures at the anode, including pure H 2, 80% 
H2 + 20% Ar, 50% H 2 + 50% Ar, and 30% H 2 + 70% Ar. The LSV curves show that 
under pure H2, the HOR current continuously increases with potential, without reaching 
a transport limiting value within the tested range, indicating fast HOR kinetics  
(Supplementary Fig. 14a). In contrast, for the H2/Ar mixtures, the polarization curves 
displayed pronounced current plateaus at higher overpotentials, characteristic of mass 
transport limitations. Notably, the onset of these plateaus shifted toward lower 
potentials and the maximum current densities decreased progressively with decreasing 
H2 concentration. These observations confirm that hydrogen concentration critically 
influences the HOR kinetics and mass transport behavior, and should be carefully 
considered in the design and operation of membrane-free systems. 
 
Besides, to address the effect of hydrogen concentration, we conducted additional HOR 
polarization tests at different H2 flow rates (5, 10, 15, and 20 sccm). At low flow rates 
(5 sccm and 10 sccm), the current density reaches a clear mass transport-limited plateau, 
indicating hydrogen supply becomes a limiting factor  (Supplementary Fig. 14b). 
However, at higher flow rates ( 15 sccm and 20 sccm, which match our long -term 
electrolysis conditions), the current density continuously increases with applied 
potential without an obvious mass transport plateau, demonstrating sufficient hydrogen 
supply and negligible mass transfer limitations under practical operating conditions. 
 
Importantly, these anode -side limitations have direct implications fo r cathodic CO 2 
reduction. As the anode becomes mass -transport-limited, the overall cell voltage 
increases, resulting in a more negative potential applied to the ECR cathode. This shift 
in cathode potential can significantly alter product selectivity by promoting undesired 
side reactions such as the hydrogen evolution reaction, thereby compromising the 
faradaic efficiency for for mic acid production. These results und erscore the necessity 
of maintaining sufficient hydrogen availability at the anode to ensure stable and 
efficient CO2 electroreduction performance in membrane-free configurations.

b) Gas crossover 
 Cathode outlet gas 
To detect the possib le gas crossover, we carried out a control experiment under non -
reactive conditions. Specifically, H2 was introduced at the anode side of a flow cell with 
the flow rate of 30 sccm, while CO2 was supplied to the cathode with the flow rate of 
20 sccm. The cell was maintained under open circuit conditions, and the gas products 
from the cathode outlet were mo nitored by gas chromatography for 10 hours. GC 
analysis was performed at 1-hour intervals throughout the 10-hour period. No H2 signal 
(retention time: 0.87 min) was detected at the cathode side , as indicated by the red -
highlighted region in the figure. (Supplementary Fig. 29), indicating that crossover of 
hydrogen from the anode to the cathode was negligible under our experimental 
configuration.

During a continuous 208-hour operation under the current density of 100 mA  cm−2 in 
ECR//HOR electrolyzer with 1  cm2 electrode area , the system exhibited stable 
performance, maintaining a HCOOH faradaic efficiency of approximately 90% 
throughout the entire duration  (Fig. 4f). These results indicate that any possible

 CO stripping tests 
To directly verify whether CO produced at the ECR cathode during long-term operation 
could diffuse to the anode and cause CO poisoning, CO stripping experiments and 
cyclic voltammetry were performed on the commercial Pt/C -based gas diffusion 
electrode after stability tests. The presence or absence of a distinct CO oxidation peak 
was used to evaluate possible CO adsorption on the Pt surface. 
 
After the stability test  (208 hours) in the flow cell, the Pt/C GDE was carefully 
transferred to a three -electrode cell using 0.5  M K 2SO4 + H 2SO4 (pH ~1.8) as the 
electrolyte. A graphite rod was used as the counter electrode and a saturated calomel 
electrode served as the reference. Cyclic voltammetry scans were first performed 
directly as shown in Supplementary Fig. 28. No CO oxidation peak was observed, 
indicating that no significant CO adsorption occurred on the Pt/C anode during long -
term operation. Subsequently, to confirm the electrode ’s activity for CO oxidation, 
high-purity CO gas was bubbled through the electrol yte for 20 minutes while holding 
to 0.05 V vs. RHE to ensure sufficient CO adsorption on catalyst surface. The CO 
stripping test was then conducted by CV from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at a scan rate 
of 20 mV s−1. A clear CO oxidation peak appeared at approximately 0.88 V during the 
first CO stripping scan and disappeared in the second scan . Abovementioned tests  
confirming that the Pt surface remained fully active for CO oxidation and was not 
poisoned by CO generated at the cathode during long -term operation1-3. These results

under our operating conditions.

Main text, Page 19 
CO stripping 
We conducted CO stripping experiments on the commercial Pt/C gas diffusion 
electrode (GDE) after the long -term stability test. After operation in the flow cell, the 
Pt/C GDE was carefully transferred to a conventional three-electrode cell using 0.5 M 
K2SO4 + H2SO4 (pH ~1.8) as the electrolyte, with a saturated calomel electrode as the 
reference electrode and a graphite rod as the counter electrode. Cyclic voltammetry (CV) 
was first performed by scanning from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at 20 mV s −1 to detect 
any CO oxidation peak. Subsequently, high -purity CO gas was bubbled through the 
electrolyte for 20 min while holding the working electrode potential at 0.05 V vs. RHE 
to ensure sufficient CO adsorption. The cell was then flushed with fresh N 2-purged 
electrolyte for 10 min to remove residual CO. Finally, CO stripping was carried out by 
CV scanning twice from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at a scan rate of 20 mV s−1. 
 
c) local pH shifts  
We agree with the reviewer that the local pH at both the anode and ca thode changes 
during electrolysis due to the in -situ generation of H + at the anode and OH − at the 
cathode. However, in our design, these locally generated ions recombine to form H 2O 
in the middle electrolyte chamber, preventing any impact on the bulk electrolyte pH. 
Consequently, the electrolyte pH remains stable during long-term electrolysis. 
 
4. Is the Bi oxidation state truly preserved under acidic bias? 
 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer  for this question. The standard redox potential of the Bi 3+/Bi0 
couple is approximately + 0.31 V vs. SHE (Small, 2023, 19, 41, 2303172; Dalton Trans., 
2018, 47, 8794 -8800). In our system, the applied potential is much more negative, 
which thermodynamically favors the reduction of Bi 3+ species to metallic Bi 0 and 
stabilizes the metallic phase during electrolysis. This is supported by our post-reaction 
characterizations: a) HRTEM reveals clear lattice fringes corresponding to Bi (015) 
planes (Supplementary Fig. 16b), indicating the presence of crystalline Bi 0 after ECR; 
b) XPS spectra show Bi 4f 7/2 and 4f 5/2 peaks at 156.4 eV and 161.7 eV , respectively 
(Supplementary Fig. 17a), which are characteristic of metallic bismuth4,5. These results 
confirm that the Bi existed in metallic phase under acidic bias.

We appreciate the reviewer’s valuable comment regarding the mechanistic role of the

performed in situ shell isolated nanoparticle-enhanced Raman spectroscopy (SHINERS) 
to directly probe the adsorption behavior of CO 2 reduction intermediates on Bi and 
Bi0.5Ag0.5 surfaces. Sequential potentials from −0.3 V to −1.0 V vs. RHE were applied.

catalyst shows a lower onset potential for CO2− formation. These results indicate that 
Bi and Bi0.5-Ag0.5 follow the same mechanism for formic aicd formation; however, the 
presence of Ag lowers the onset potential due to enhanced conductivity.

6. Supplying pure H 2 to the anode may not be economically viable for large -scale 
systems. Authors should at least discuss alternative hydrogen sources (e.g., internal 
HER coupling) or system integration strategies. 
 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for the valuable suggestion regarding the feasibility of 
integrating an internal hydrogen generation strategy within the ECR//HOR system.  
While H 2 was externally supplied to the anode in the present study, we have also 
demonstrated th e feasibility of on -site H 2 generation using a separate methanol 
oxidation-HER system (Fig. 5), in which H 2 is produced at the cathode via HER, and 
MOR at the anode side. We have included additional discussion and comparison in the 
Supplementary Information, also copied below.

Stability test of the integrated ECR//HOR and HER//AEM//MOR system . c, FEHCOOH 
in the ECR//HOR electrolyzer and FEformate in the HER//AEM//MOR electrolyzer. d, 
TEA analysis of the integrated system for HCOOH/formate production. 
 
Supplementary Information, Page 44 
Supplementary Note. 1 | Different H2 source. 
In our system, the design forms a modular and integrated H2 cycle, in which the 
hydrogen produced from HER is immediately reused in the ECR//HOR electrolyzer, 
forming a closed-loop H2 cycle without reliance on external gas inputs. Although the 
HER and HOR units are not physically co -located within a single reactor, the setup 
demonstrates a practical and scalable example of internal hydrogen integration, which 
could be further refined into a spatially compact architecture in future system designs. 
Compared to other anodic reactions commonly paired with HER, such as water 
oxidation (OER), urea oxidation (UOR), or other organic oxidations —methanol 
oxidation (MOR) offers several key advantages: a much lower onset potential (typically 
0−0.2 V vs. RHE), enabling lower overall cell voltage; abundant and low-cost feedstock 
(liquid methanol); high-purity hydrogen generation without the need for gas separation; 
greater compatibility with HOR-based cathodic reactions in modular architectures.. 
While internal HER −HOR coupling has been proposed in some designs, it typically 
requires gas separation and recirculation infrastructure, which may complicate system 
engineering. In contrast, our approach leverages electrochemical pairing between MOR 
and HER in one unit, and HOR −ECR in the other, creating an efficient and modular 
architecture

7. To highlight the advantage of the membrane-free acidic system, it would be 
informative to include direct performance comparisons (FE, energy efficiency, voltage) 
with neutral or alkaline membrane-based ECR systems using the same Bi–Ag catalyst. 
 
Response:  
We appreciate the reviewer’s suggestions. To clarify the performance advantage of the 
membrane-free acidic ECR//HOR configuration, we have conducted 8 h stability tests 
using the Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst in the two-electrode membrane-based ECR//CEM//HOR 
electrolyzer (with a total electrolyte flow layer thickness of 2 mm). T wo different 
catholytes were used: 100 mL 0.5 M K2SO4 or 1 M KOH. Note that the Pt/C anode was 
in direct contact with the Nafion membrane, and no anolyte was used between the anode 
and the CEM. During the 8 h electrolysis, the pH value of the catholyte was recorded 
every hour using a pH meter. At the same intervals, the catholyte were collected for 
liquid-phase product analysis via NMR spectroscopy. Simultaneously, the outlet gas 
stream from the cathode was analyzed by GC to quantify the gas-phase products. 
 
At a current densit y of 100 mA cm −2, the faradaic efficiency for formate remained 
around 90% in both electrolytes (Supplementary Fig. 26), demonstrating comparable 
selectivity to that in the acidic membrane-free system. However, the cell voltages in 
neutral (2.2 V) and alkaline (2.7 V) electrolytes in ECR//CEM//HOR electrolyzer were 
significantly higher than that in the membrane -free acidic ECR//HOR system (1.7 V), 
underscoring the energy advantage of the me mbrane-free acidic configuration for 
efficient CO2 electroreduction. 
 
Our findings show that while the Bi0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst maintains comparable selectivity 
in neutral and alkaline electrolyte, the acidic membrane -free system offers a lower 
operating voltage, owing to the minimized ohmic/membrane resistance.

Main text, Page 12 
To highlight the advantage of the membrane-free acidic ECR//HOR configuration, we 
also evaluated its performance against membrane -based systems using neutral (0.5 M 
K2SO4) and alkaline (1 M KOH) electrolytes (Supplementary Fig. 26). Although all 
systems showed similar formate  selectivity (~90%), the membrane -free acidic setup

Main text, Page 7: 
We selected Bi -based materials as the catalyst due to its low toxicity and high 
abundance, which also exhibit high formate selectivity 43. However, the electrical 
conductivity of Bi is relatively low, leading to the higher cell voltage. Considering this, 
we introduced high electrical conductive silver into the Bi -based materials to enhance 
the charge transfer and lower the ECR potentials44. We synthesized different composed 
Bi-Ag and pure Bi through grinding method. According to the different precursor 
stoichiometric ratios, the catalysts are designated as Bi, Bi 0.3-Ag0.7, Bi0.5-Ag0.5, Bi0.7-
Ag0.3, respectively. Scanning electron microscopy (SEM) and transmission electron 
microscopy (TEM) images show that Ag nanoparticles are dispersed uniformly on the 
Bi nanosheets in the as-prepared samples and EDS mapping demonstrates the uniform 
distribution of Bi and Ag within the structure (Fig. 2b-c, Supplementary Figs. 2-6).  X-
ray diffraction (XRD) patterns confirm the co -existence of metallic Bi and Ag in the 
structure for all Bi-Ag samples (Fig. 2d). X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS) also 
confirmed the existence of Bi and Ag (Supplementary Fig. 7). Bi0.5-Ag0.5 electrocatalyst 
with efficient charge transfer capability, exhibiting high activity, selectivity, and 
stability for CO 2-to-HCOOH conversion was designed (details for electrocatalysts 
design are discussed in Supplementary Note 2, Supplement ary Figs. 8 -10). The 
FEHCOOH remained above 90% across a current density range of 100−400 mA cm−2, 
with a HCOOH partial current density reaching 364 mA cm −2 at −1.31 V vs. RHE in 
acidic electrolyte (Fig. 2e, 2f). The ECR performance of the Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst was 
also assessed in neutral and alkaline electrolytes, where formate Faradaic efficiencies 
consistently exceeded 90% (Supplementary Fig. 12).

The HOR polarization curves under different H 2 flow rates and H 2/Ar ratios were

Ag/AgCl, and Bi0.5-Ag0.5 were used as the working electrode, reference electrode, and 
counter electrode, respectively. Polarization curves were performed from 0 to 1.6 V (V 
vs. RHE) at a scan rate of 20 mV/s. The electrolyte was 0.5 M K 2SO4 adjusted with 
H2SO4, and the electrolyte flow rate was maintained at 8 mL/min. The ECR 
performance was evaluated under different H2 flow rates (5, 10, 15, and 20 sccm) using 
a two -electrode configuration.  Moreover, polarization curves were conducted in a 
three-electrode system under different H2/Ar gas, including pure H2, 80% H2 + 20% Ar, 
50% H2 + 50% Ar, and 30% H2 + 70% Ar. In addition, ECR performance was evaluated 
in a two -electrode system at a current density of 100 mA cm −2 under the same gas 
conditions. 
 
For the long -term stability tests of the ECR//HOR electrolyzer, electrochemical 
measurements were performed in a two-electrode configuration using flow cells with 
electrode areas of either 0.5 cm × 2 cm or 2 cm × 2 cm. The corresponding electrolyte 
flow layer thicknesses were 2 mm and 3 mm, respectively. Electrolyte volumes of 
500 mL (1 cm2 cell) and 1000 mL (4  cm2 cell) were circulated at flow rates of 8 mL 
min−1 and 5 mL min−1, respectively, and refreshed every 24 hours to maintain stability. 
In both setups, the CO2 feed was humidified by bubbling through HCl solutions (0.05 M 
for the 1 cm 2 cell and 0.2 M for the 4 cm 2 cell) prior to entering the cathode 
compartment8. CO2 flow rates were set at 20 sccm and 30 sccm for the 1 cm2 and 4 cm2 
cells, respectively. 
 
We conducted CO stripping experiments on the commercial Pt/C gas diffusion 
electrode (GDE) after the long-term stability test. After operation in the flow cell, the 
Pt/C GDE was carefully transferred to a conventional three -electrode cell using 0.5 M 
K2SO4 + H2SO4 (pH ~1.8) as the electrolyte, with a saturated calomel electrode as the 
reference electrode and a graphite rod as the counter electrode. Cyclic voltammetry (CV) 
was first performed by scanning from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at 20 mV s −1 to detect 
any CO oxidation peak. Subsequently, high -purity CO gas was bubbled through the 
electrolyte for 20 min while holding the working electrode potential at 0.05 V vs. RHE 
to ensure sufficient CO adsorption. The cell was then flushed with fresh N 2-purged

Reviewer #2 
 
This manuscript reported a membrane -free reactor for CO 2 reduction in an acidic 
electrolyte. Compared with membrane-based electrolyzers, the membrane -free design 
maintains a stable electrolyte pH, which is crucial for acidic CO2 reduction. To address 
challenges associated with this design, they utilized electro chemical hydrogen 
oxidation to replace the oxygen evolution reaction, thereby suppressing formic acid 
overoxidation at the anode. Additionally, they proposed coupling methanol oxidation 
with the hydrogen evolution reaction to reduce H 2 costs. As a result, the reactor 
exhibited high selectivity, single-pass efficiency, and stability. More impressively, they 
emphasized the often -overlooked significance of proton transfer efficiency across the 
membrane, which greatly affects the pH of the catholyte. This is im portant for 
accurately evaluate acidic CO2 reduction performance and maintain long-term stability. 
Therefore, this manuscript could be accepted for publication in Nature 
Communications after addressing the following questions. 
 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for their positive comments and constructive 
suggestions. We have carried out additional CO 2 electroreduction tests under neutral 
and alkaline conditions to demonstrate the catalyst ’s performance, optimized the 
catalyst reaction conditions to extend the long-term stability to 208 hours, and included 
an updated techno -economic analysis with revised formic acid separation costs. We 
have also added a detailed comparison with recent membrane -free and membrane -
based electrolyzers to highlight the advantages of our system in selectivity and stability. 
These new results and discussions have been incorporated into the revised manuscript. 
We believe these improvements comprehensively address the reviewer’s concerns and 
further strengthen the impact and practical significance of our work. 
 
1. The authors used the same pH solution as both the anolyte and catholyte. However, 
some acidic CO2 reduction studies use a pure acid solution as the anolyte. What is the 
influence of the anolyte composition? 
 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for the insightful comment. The composition of the 
anolyte and catholyte, including the anolyte species  (pH, with or without alkaline 
cations), concentration, as well as the concentration gradient between the anolyte and 
catholyte, significantly influence  Ji, diffusion . In other words, the anolyte can directly 
affect the catholyte composition and pH.  Although the anolyte is separated from the 
cathode by a membrane  in previous membrane -based design , it still impacts the

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 29 -->

P
A
G
E
2
3
68 
 
cathodic CO2 reduction reaction through proton crossover and pH drift. A pure acidic 
anolyte may enhance H + flux to the cathode, helpi ng maintain a low catholyte pH; 
however, excessive proton transport can result in 𝜂𝐻+  exceeding 100%, leading to 
continuous pH decline at the cathode, which in turn promotes HER and suppresses CO2 
reduction selectivity. In contrast, our membrane -free design enables strict control of  
𝜂𝐻+ at 100%, maintaining a stable electrolyte pH throughout operation. 
 
2. The authors developed BiAg catalyst for CO 2-to-formic acid conversion. How does 
its performance compare with previ ously reported catalysts? Additionally, how does 
it’s performance in neutral and alkaline electrolytes? 
 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for the valuable question regarding the performance 
comparison of our Bi -Ag catalyst.  To address this point, we conducted additional 
experiments using a three -electrode flow cell system to evaluate the intrinsic CO2 
reduction performance of the Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst in both neutral (0.5 M K 2SO4) and 
alkaline (1 M KOH) electrolytes  with an ECR//CEM//OER electrolyzer. As shown in 
Supplementary Fig. 12, the catalyst exhibited excellent ECR selectivity across a wide 
range of current densities (100−400 mA cm−2). In both electrolytes, the formic acid FEs 
remained above 90%, demonstrating the versatility of the Bi0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst in 
different pH environments.  
 
In addition, our Bi0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst delivers a Faradaic efficiency for formic acid over 
90% at 100 mA cm −2 in acidic electrolytes, while maintaining stability for 208 hours. 
Several previously reported systems have demonstrated CO2-to-formic acid electrolysis 
at higher current densities (Supplementary Table 4); however, their operation lifetimes 
under acidic c onditions are typically limited to several tens of hours due to catalyst 
deactivation or system instability. In contrast, the extended durability of our catalyst 
under acidic conditions at industrially relevant current densities underscores its 
potential for practical large-scale application.

3. In the membrane -based electrolyzer design, why does the catholyte pH stabilize at 
~7-8 after several hours of testing? 
 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful question. In the membrane-based electrolyzer, 
the catholyte pH initially increases during electrolysis due to the consumption of 
protons and generation of hydroxide ions at the cathode during CO2 reduction. However, 
after several hours, the catholyte pH stabilizes around 7 -8 due to the formation of a 
bicarbonate/carbonate buffer system. As CO 2 dissolves in the aqueous electrolyte, it 
reacts with OH− generated at the cathode to form HCO 3−, thereby establishing a 
chemical buffer that resists further pH change. Additionally, in the case of CEM-based 
systems, the migration of H + from the anode to the cathode further contributes to 
moderating the catholyte pH, preventing it from rising to strongly alkaline values. The 
combined effects of buffering reactions and membrane ion transport explain the 
observed pH stabilization. 
 
4. The authors reported a 50 -hour acidic stability. How does this result compare with 
current literature values? 
 
Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer ’s comment regarding the comparison with literature -
reported stability data. In our revised study, we optimized the electrolysis conditions to 
further enhance system durability under acidic environments. Specifically, to suppress 
carbonate precipitation and maintain stable operation, the CO2 feed was humidified by 
passing through a glass bubbler containing 0.05 M HCl prior to entering the cathode 
chamber of the ECR//HOR electrolyzer (Science 2025, 388, 6752, eadr3834). The 
introduction of volatile acids helps convert carbonate into soluble po tassium salts, 
thereby mitigating pore blockage. 
 
Electrolysis tests were conducted using gas diffusion electrodes (GDEs) with exposed 
areas of 1 cm 2 (electrolyzer dimension: 0.5 cm × 2 cm) and 4 cm 2 (electrolyzer 
dimension: 2 cm × 2 cm). Catalysts were uniformly spray -coated to ensure complete 
active area coverage and direct exposure to both gas and electrolyte phases during 
operation. Under these optimized conditions, the system achieved continuous operation 
for 208 hours at a current d ensity of 100 mA cm −2, with the Faradaic efficiency for 
HCOOH stably maintained at ~  90% in the 1 cm 2 cell. This significantly exceeds the 
previously reported 50-hour stability and underscores the robustness of our membrane-

free design. Additionally, in a 4 cm2 electrolyzer, similar performance (~  90% FE for 
HCOOH) was sustained for 48 hours. 
 
We have included a detailed comparison of our stability data with representative state-
of-the-art acidic ECR systems in the revised manuscript (see Supplementary Table 4). 
It shows that our system delivers comparable or superior long-term stability and product 
selectivity relative to most state-of-the-art reports.

Supplementary Information, Page 51 
a) Liquid separation cost in ECR//HOR electrolyzer 
For the liquid product separation assumption, a pressurized extraction -distillation 
method was adopted based on a previously reported process design 7. In this approach, 
the formic acid -containing electrolyte is first processed through an extraction 
distillation column, followed by solvent recovery in a second column.  Through this 
two-step separation, formic acid is concentrated to over 95 wt%, meeting commercial 
purity standa rds. All process parameters and energy consumption estimates were 
referenced from the literature7. The estimated energy consumption for the two columns 
is approximately 1097.2 kWh and 50.1 kWh, respectively.

Supplementary Information, Page 58 
b) Liquid separation cost in HER//AEM//MOR electrolyzer 
For the alkaline HER//AEM//MOR system, the primary product is formate rather than 
formic acid. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin21, the purification of formate 
involves electrodialysis followed by distillation. The capital expenditure (CAPEX) 
associated with the electrodialysis and distillation processes is estimate d at 0.06 USD 
kg−1 to deliver at least 85 wt% formic acid , while the operating expenditures (OPEX) 
for electrodialysis and distillation are 0.13 USD kg−1 and 0.12 USD kg−1, respectively. 
The associated cost of liquid-phase separation is therefore estimated with reference to 
the separation cost calculations reported in that work.

Reviewer #3  
 
In this study, the authors propose a membrane-free electrolyzer to enhance the stability 
and economic viability of electrocatalytic CO 2 reduction in acidic systems. 
Experimental results demonstrate that the membrane -free electrolyzer significantly 
improves operational stability from 8 hours to 50 hours. To further optimize the system's 
economic efficiency, the authors employ hydrogen oxidation reaction (HOR) to replace 
the conventional oxygen evolution reaction (OER) at the anode and introduce a tandem 
reaction strategy (HOR-CEM-MOR) to reduce overall operational costs. Upon careful 
evaluation, the reviewer considers the current study pres ents critical shortcomings in 
practical applicability and data accuracy that undermine the validity of its conclusions. 
Specific concerns are outlined below: 
 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for highlighting concerns about the techno -economic 
analysis, practical applicability, and data accuracy. To address these issues 
comprehensively, we have made the following major revisions: 
 
First, we have significantly improved the TEA section. Specifically, we recalculated the 
cost of formic acid separation by including detailed estimates for purifying formic acid 
to certain concentration in the acidic electrolyzer and the alkaline HER//AEM//MOR 
configuration. We have also added a new table summarizing all key parameters and 
calculation assumptions to ensure clarity and reproducibility of the economic analysis. 
 
In addition , we conducted additional experiments to verify long -term stability and 
system feasibility. We performed an 8-hour stability test for the CRR//CEM//OER cell 
with a 2 mm intermediate layer, and we further optimized the membrane -free 
electrolyzer operation to achieve stable performance for 208 hours and 48 hours at the 
current density of 100 mA cm−2 in the electrolyzer with the electrode area of 1 cm2 and 
4 cm2 under the optimized testing conditions, respectively. In addition, we confirmed 
that there is no significant anode-to-cathode gas crossover during extended operation. 
 
We believe these comprehensive updates to the TEA, along with the new experimental 
validation, effectively address the reviewer ’s concerns and enhance the practical 
relevance of our conclusions.

2. To fairly highlight the advantages of the membrane -free strategy, comparative 
analyses under equivalent conditions are imperative. Notably, the parameters for the 
CRR//CEM//OER process were derived from a 10 -mm flow channel plate, whereas 
those for CRR//HOR and CRR/CEM/MOR were based on a 2 -mm plate. This 
discrepancy invalidates direct economic comparisons. 
 
Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer ’s insightful comment regarding the inconsistency in the 
electrolyzer configuration, which indeed complicates a direct techno -economic 
comparison. To address this concern, we conducted additional electrolysis experiments 
for the CRR//CEM//OER system using the same 2-mm flow channel plate as employed 
in the CRR//HOR and CRR//CEM//MOR setups. The 8-hour stability test revealed that 
the HCOOH FE in the CRR//CEM//OER configuration is significantly lower than that 
observed in the CRR//H OR system under identical conditions  (Supplementary Fig. 
27). This performance decline is primarily attributed to carbonate accumulation at the 
cathode, which is severe under OER operation and leads to cathode flooding and active 
site blockage.

The results have been incorporated into the revised manuscript on page 12. 
Moreover, we performed electrochemical measurements in ECR//CEM//OER 
electrolyzer using the same 2 -mm flow channel and observed high cell voltage and a 
notable decrease in HCOOH FE compared to the CRR//HOR system, primarily due to 
severe carbonate accumulation at the cathode (Supplementary Fig. 27). 
 
3. As the authors emphasize that acidic systems avoid the acidification and separation 
costs associated wi th alkaline -derived formate. However, the CRR//CEM//MOR 
configuration employs an alkaline electrolyte, this introduces additional formic acid 
purification expenses, which are not considered in the current TEA. 
 
Response:   
We sincerely thank the reviewer for raising this important concern regarding the techno-
economic analysis. We have accounted for the separation costs of formate and have 
reanalyzed the TEA, as presented below. 
 
Supplementary Information, Page 58 
For the alkaline HER//AEM//MOR system, the primary product is formate rather than 
formic acid. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin21, the purification of formate 
involves electrodialysis followed by distillation. The capital expenditure (CAPEX) 
associated with the electrodialysis and distillation processes is estimated at 0.06 USD 
kg−1 to deliver at least 85 wt% formic acid , while the operating expenditures (OPEX)  
for electrodialysis and distillation are 0.13 USD kg−1 and 0.12 USD kg−1, respectively. 
The associated cost of liquid-phase separation is therefore estimated with reference to 
the separation cost calculations reported in that work.

We have made the following revision in the main text on page 15. 
 
Main text, Page 14: 
A comprehensive TEA was carried out  (Supplementary Note 3 and Tables 5 -6). 
Assuming a CO2 feedstock cost of 40 USD ton−1 and a methanol feedstock cost of 470 
USD ton −1, and an electricity price of 0.03 USD kWh −1, the estimated  levelized 
production cost of formic acid (HCOOH) is approximately 859 USD ton −1 (Fig. 5d). 
The major cost components contributing to this value include: separation cost, 
electrolyzer capital cost , and electricity expense. A pressurized extraction–distillation 
method was used for the ECR//HOR system, whereas electrodialysis followed by 
distillation was employed for the HER//AEM//MOR system.When the electricity price 
decreases to 0.02 USD kWh −1, the HCOOH production cost can be further reduced to 
830 USD ton −1 (Supplementary Fig. 39), which is comparable to the current market 
price of 830 USD ton−1, highlighting the economic viability of this integrated system.   
 
4. Carbonate formation typically limits CO2 reduction stability to <200 h due to pore-
blocking effects in CO2 transport channels. Despite adopting a membrane-free system, 
the reported stability (50 h) remains insufficient to demonstrate practical superiority. 
Furthermore, this limited stability raises concerns about operational feasibility in real-
world applications. 
 
Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer’s insightful comment regarding the long -term operational 
stability of our membrane -free CO2 reduction system. We conducted additional

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 42 -->

P
A
G
E
3
6
68 
 
electrolysis experiments under optimized conditions to directly address this concern. 
Specifically, to further suppress carbonate precipitation, the CO2 feed gas was first 
humidified by passing it through a glass bubbler containing 0.05 M HCl solution before 
introduction into the cathode chamber of the flow cell  (Science 2025, 388, 6752, 
eadr3834). The presence of volatile acids can facilitate the conversion of carbonates 
into more soluble potassium salts.  The CO2 flow rate was controlled at 20 sccm  
throughout the test. 
 
With the optimized conditions, the membrane-free electrolyzer with an electrode area 
of 2 cm × 0.5 cm exhibited stable operation for over 208 hours, maintaining a Faradaic 
efficiency for HCOOH above 90% (Fig. 4f). This greatly surpasses previously reported 
stability durations (~50 hours), indicating that carbonate-induced pore blockage can be 
effectively suppressed. Furthermore, the scaled -up 2 cm × 2 cm electrolyzer also 
achieved stable operation for approximately 48 hours with  a comparable HCOOH 
Faradaic efficiency of around 90%  (Supplementary Fig. 25), confirming the 
robustness and scalability of the acidic membrane -free configuration for extended 
operation. 
 
To further substantiate these findings, we have compared our long -term stability data 
with representative state-of-the-art CO2 reduction systems reported in the literature (see 
Supplementary Table 4). Our system achieves competitive or superior performance in 
terms of both operational duration and product selectivity. 
 
Main text, Page 12 
To further reduce product cost, we designed an electrolyzer with a reduced electrolyte 
flow layer thickness, decreasing the distance between the anode and cathode from 10 
mm to 2 mm , which effectively lowers cell resistance and thus the cell voltage  
(Supplementary Fig. 21). As a result, the cell voltage of the ECR//HOR electrolyzer 
decreased from 3 V to approximately 1.7 V at 100 mA cm −2 (Fig. 4d). The optimized 
system exhibited stable operation for 50 hours at 100 mA cm−2, maintaining a FEHCOOH 
of 90%. It continuously produced HCOOH at a rate of 0.008 M L−1 h−1 (Supplementary 
Fig. 22), and the maximum HCOOH concentration reached 0.1 M, demonstrating the 
practical potential of our design. Post-analysis of the cathode electrode after 50 hours 
revealed minor salt precipitation (Fig. 4e, Supplementary Fig. 23), indicating that the 
optimized membrane -free ECR//HOR electrolyzer effectively mitigates severe 
carbonate accumulation e ven under prolonged operation.  However, the minor salt 
precipitation obstructed the gas flow tubing. To address this issue, we used acidic 
humidification strategy55, which enabled stable CO2 reduction for over 208 hours (Figs. 
4f, Supplementary Table 4). Furthermore, long-term electrolysis conducted in a 4 cm 2

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 43 -->

P

68 
 
flow electrolyzer (2 cm × 2 cm electrode area) also demonstrated stable performance, 
maintaining a HCOOH FE of approximately 90% over a 48 -hour operation period 
(Supplementary Figs. 24-25). 
 
Main text, Page 19 
For the long -term stability tests of the ECR//HOR electrolyzer, electrochemical 
measurements were performed in a two -electrode configuration using flow cells with 
electrode areas of either 0.5 cm × 2 cm or 2 cm × 2 cm. The corresponding elec trolyte 
flow layer thicknesses were 2 mm and 3 mm, respectively. Electrolyte volumes of 
500 mL (1 cm2 cell) and 1000 mL (4 cm 2 cell) were circulated at flow rates of 8 mL 
min−1 and 5 mL min−1, respectively, and refreshed every 24 hours to maintain stability. 
In both setups, the CO2 feed was humidified by bubbling through HCl solutions (0.05 M 
for the 1 cm 2 cell and 0. 2 M for the 4 cm 2 cell) prior to entering the cathode 
compartment54. CO2 flow rates were set at 20 sccm and 30 sccm for the smaller and 
larger cells, respectively.

5. The reviewer questions whether reactant crossover from the anode to the cathode via 
the gas  diffusion layer (GDL) in the membrane -free system would increase gas 
separation costs, a factor omitted in the analysis. 
 
Response:  
We appreciate the reviewer ’s insightful comment on potential reactant crossover.  To 
detect the possib le gas crossover, we carried out a control experiment under non -
reactive conditions. Specifically, H2 was introduced at the anode side of a flow cell with 
the flow rate of 30 sccm, while CO 2 was supplied to the cathode with the flow rate of 
20 sccm. The cell was maintained und er open circuit conditions, and the gas products 
from the cathode outlet were monitored by gas chromatography for 10 hours. GC 
analysis was performed at 1-hour intervals throughout the 10-hour period. No H2 signal 
was detected at the cathode side, as indicated by the red-highlighted region in the figure.  
(Supplementary Fig. 29), indicating that crossover of hydrogen from the anode to the 
cathode was negligible under our experimental configuration.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 45 -->

68 
 
 
Supplementary Fig. 29 | GC analysis of the cathodic outlet stream over 10 hours 
under non-electrochemical conditions. H2 was continuously supplied to the anode 
side, and CO2 to the cathode side of the flow cell. No H2 (retention time: 0.87 min) was 
detected in the cathodic outlet, indicating negligible hydrogen crossover through the 
membrane. 
 
Page 13 in main text: 
The H2 permeation test also indicated that H 2 on the anode side scarcely crossed the 
electrolyte to reach the cathode (Supplementary Fig. 29). 
 
To estimate the baseline cost of product gas separation, we referred to established 
industrial data for biogas upgrading via pressure swing adsorption (PSA),  which 
involves a similar separation of CO 2 from other gas components  (Ind. Eng. Chem. 
Res. 2018, 57, 6, 2165−2177). Based on these industrial benchmarks, a representative 
capital cost of approximately 1,989,043 USD per 1,000 m3·h−1 of capacity is adopted, 
with a capacity scaling factor of 0.7. The operating cost is assumed to consist solely of 
electricity consumption, estimated at 0.25 kWh per cubic meter of processed gas. It 
should be noted that subsequent compression and storage requirements,  if any, have 
been excluded from this cost estimate.

7. The electrocatalytic reaction area is not explicitly stated. Detailed experimental 
information, including electrode dimensions and active surface area, must be provided. 
 
Response:  
We appreciate the reviewer ’s suggestion regarding the need to provide detailed 
information about the electrocatalytic reaction area. In the revised manuscript, we have 
added a clear description of the electrode dimensions and the corresponding active 
surface area for all electrochemical tests. 
 
Specifically, the gas diffusion electrodes used for ECR were prepared with an exposed 
geometric area of 1 cm 2 (electrolyzer dimension: 0.5 cm × 2 cm) for standard 
measurements and 4 cm2 (electrolyzer dimension: 2 cm × 2 cm) for scale-up validation 
tests. The catalyst was uniformly sprayed onto the GDE to ensure full coverage of the 
active area. The electrochemical cell design ensured that the entire catalyst-coated area 
was in direct contact with the electrolyte and gas flow during operation. 
 
The updated details have been added to the main text, including electrode preparation, 
dimensions, and any relevant surface area characterizations:

flow la yer thicknesses were 2 mm and 3 mm, respectively. Electrolyte volumes of 
500 mL (1 cm2 cell) and 1000 mL (4 cm 2 cell) were circulated at flow rates of 8 mL 
min−1 and 5 mL min−1, respectively, and refreshed every 24 hours to maintain stability.

This article by Da et al. showed a detailed study on using a membrane-free electrolyzer 
design to manage pH at the cathode and hence mitigate salt precipitation during CO2 
electrolysis, which is a major challenge in the field. They systematically compared the 
effect of pH stabilization with different electrode pairing strategies (ECR/CEM/OER, 
ECR/CEM/HOR, ECR/OER, and ECR/HOR), identifying the membraneless ECR/HOR 
as the most promising configuration in terms of formic acid faradaic efficiency, proton 
transfer efficiency, pH management, and cell voltage, etc. They also demonstrated a 
cascade of ECR/HOR and ECR/AEM/MOR electrolyzers that show promise for 
practical application based on the TEA. Although the concept of membrane -free 
CER/HOR electrolyzers is not new (see Fu et al., Science 379, 707–712 (2023); Pang 
et al. Joule 6, 12, 2745 - 2761; and Lazouski et al. Nat Catal 3, 463–469 (2020), none 
of which were cited), the authors added value by comparing the different cell 
configurations and extending to a tan dem scheme while achieving appreciable 
performance in terms of current density, Faradaic efficiency, and single pass 
conversion efficiency. The study presented here should be of interest to the 
electrochemistry and the broader CO2 utilization communities. However, the following 
concerns need to be addressed before the article is suitable for publication. 
 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for the constructive comments and for pointing out the 
relevant literature.  
1. We have now properly cited the suggested references (Science 2023, 379, 707−712; 
Joule 2022, 6, 2745−2761; Nat. Catal. 2020, 3, 463−469) in the revised manuscript 
as Ref. 50, Ref. 21., Ref. 5 1, respectively, to better acknowledge pioneering work 
on membrane-free ECR//HOR electrolyzer concepts.  
2. Following the reviewer ’s valuable suggestions, we have strengthened the techno -
economic analysis. Specifically, we have recalculated the formic acid separation 
cost, conducted a new cash flow analysis,  and performed a comprehensive 
sensitivity analysis. These results are now clearly presented in the revised TEA 
section and summarized in a new supplementary table for transparency.  
3. We performed additional electrochemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS) 
measurements for ECR//HOR cells with different intermediate layer thicknesses to 
elucidate resistance differences and better explain the origin of the observed cell 
voltage trends.  
4. We have optimized the operating conditions to extend the stability test of the  
membrane-free ECR//HOR cell under acidic conditions to 208 h using a 2 cm × 
0.5 cm electrode, and we also conducted long-term tests with larger electrodes (2 cm

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 51 -->
