5
68 
 
× 2 cm) to further validate the practical feasibility of the system.  
5. To address concerns abou t possible Pt poisoning by CO, we performed additional 
electrochemical measurements confirming that CO poisoning does not occur under 
our operating conditions. 
 
1. The TEA looks promising and can be helpful for projecting the competitive advantage 
of the new technology, but I have some questions and concerns. (i) A more detailed 
written description of the assumptions for “the liquid product separation” is required. 
Currently, the method(s) of separation are not described, key assumptions about the 
concentration of the solutions entering and leaving the separation unit are absent, and 
there are no citations for the Capex and operating costs. For the tandem process 
involving formate production from MOR, was the liquid separation cost for the MOR -
derived formate accounted for as well? Salty, alkaline formate/formic acid will have 
very little value. (ii) The TEA should be carried out as a discounted cash flow analysis 
using a typical discount rate (e.g. 10%). It seems that this was not done. (iii) Was the 
electrolyzer Capex of $919/m^2, derived from a value of $250 $/kW (which is very low), 
applied to both the water electrolyzer and the CO2 electrolyzer? This should be clearly 
stated. It’s also not clear if the 250 $/kW is for a PEM or liquid alkaline electrolyze r. 
(iv) It would be very helpful to briefly state some of the key assumptions for the TEA in 
the main article (e.g., cost of electrolyzer, Capex, method of separation, price of CO2, 
price of MeOH) so that the reader can better understand the baseline scena rio being 
presented. Optional: a sensitivity analysis would be helpful. 
 
Response:  
We appreciate the reviewer’s comment related with techno-economic analysis. We have 
revised the TEA according to your suggestion, please check the revised Supplementary 
Information, also copied below. 
 
Supplementary Information, Page 50 
1) Separation cost  
a) Liquid separation cost in ECR//HOR electrolyzer 
For the liquid product separation assumption, a pressurized extraction -distillation 
method was adopted based on a previously reported process design 7. In this approach, 
the formic acid -containing electrolyte is first processed through an extraction 
distillation column, followed by solvent recovery in a second column.  Through this 
two-step separation, formic acid is concentrated to over 95 wt%, meeting commercial 
purity standards. All process parameters and energy consumption estimates were 
referenced from the literature7. The estimated energy consumption for the two columns 
is approximately 1097.2 kWh and 50.1 kWh, respectively.

b) Liquid separation cost in HER//AEM//MOR electrolyzer 
For the alkaline HER//AEM//MOR system, the primary product is formate rather than 
formic acid. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin21, the purification of formate 
involves electrodialysis followed by distillation. The capital expenditure (CAPEX) 
associated with the electrodialysis and di stillation processes is estimated at 0.06 USD 
kg−1 to deliver at least 85 wt% formic acid , while the operating expenditures (OPEX) 
for electrodialysis and distillation are 0.13 USD kg−1 and 0.12 USD kg−1, respectively.

Calculation of net present value  
The net present value at the end of the facility’s service life is evaluated by discounting 
the projected annual cash flows over the  entire operational period. The total capital 
expenditure accounts for all fixed capital investments, with a corporate tax rate of 38.9% 
and a nominal discount rate of 10% applied ( Ind. Eng. Chem. Res.  2018, 57, 6, 
2165−2177). In this analysis, working cap ital is assumed to be 5% of the total capital 
cost, and the plant is projected to retain a residual salvage value equal to 20% of its 
initial capital investment upon decommissioning. 
In year zero, the facility is built, leading to a cumulative present value of:

3) Electrolyzer capex  
We appreciate the reviewer ’s valuable comment highlighting the need to clarify how 
the electrolyzer capital cost was determined and applied in our analysis. 
 
At present, no large -scale commercial CO 2 electrolyzer installations exist, so there is 
no established industrial benchmark for CO 2 electrolyzer stack costs. To address this 
gap, we adopted a capital cost estimate based on mature water electrolyzer technologies 
as a proxy, following common practice in TEA. Specifically: 
 
For the CO 2 electrolyzer, we used a proton exchange membrane (PEM) water 
electrolyzer as the reference technology due to its comparable stack configuration, 
catalyst layers, and balance-of-plant requirements. According to recent DOE H2A and 
NREL reports, the PEM electrolyzer stack cost is typically around 560 USD kW −1,19 
which includes all major cost elements such as platinum and iridium catalyst layers. 
For our CO 2-to-formate scenario, the cathodic catalyst (e.g., bismuth) and anodic 
catalyst (e.g., platinum) are assumed to be comparable in cost and loading to the 
platinum and iridium used in PEM water electrolysis. To convert this reference cost to

an area -specific value, we used the representative operating conditions for a PEM 
electrolyzer: current density = 0.1 7 A cm−2, cell voltage = 1.7 V 9,10. The conversion is 
calculated as follows: 
𝐶𝑜𝑠𝑡𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑙𝑦𝑧𝑒𝑟  ( USD

This updated value supersedes the earlier 919 USD/m2, which was based on an outdated 
lower stack cost. 
 
Separately, for the HER//CEM//MOR system in our comparison, the stack cost of 
250.25 USD kW−1 was applied based on the DOE H2A central grid alkaline electrolysis 
reference24 (Norsk Hydro HPE Atmospheric Type No. 5040) operated at 1.75 V and 
0.175 A cm−2, as documented in references 25,26. This value represents a conventional 
liquid alkaline electrolyzer, not PEM. 
 
In summary, the area-specific CAPEX for the CO 2 electrolyzer was derived using the 
PEM reference cost (now updated to 1618.4  USD/m2), while the HER//CEM//MOR 
configuration uses the alkaline reference cost (250.25  USD/kW). The two are applied 
separately as appropriate in our TEA model. 
 
Supplementary Information, Page 47 
At present, there is no large -scale commercial deployment of CO 2 electrolyzers, and 
consequently, no well-established capital cost data are available for industrial-scale CO2 
electrolysis stacks. To address this, our analysis adopts a cost model analogous to that 
of proton exchange membrane (PEM) water electrolysis sys tems, which represent the 
most comparable mature electrochemical technology in terms of stack design, catalyst 
configuration, and balance-of-plant requirements. 
According to the most recent assessments by the National Renewable Energy 
Laboratory (NREL), th e cost basis for PEM electrolyzers encompasses all relevant 
plant components, including the electrolyzer stack, catalyst layers (platinum and 
iridium for the cathode and anode, respectively), and associated balance -of-plant 
equipment. In the context of CO2 electroreduction to formate, the cathodic catalyst (e.g., 
bismuth) and anodic catalyst (e.g., platnium) are assumed to be of comparable cost and 
loading to the platinum and iridium catalysts used in PEM water electrolysis. As such,

the capital cost of the CO2 electrolyzer stack is reasonably approximated by a reference 
value of 560 USD/kW 9. For the reference water electrolyzer, the current density is 
0.17A/cm2 with a cell voltage of 1.7 V9,10. To express this unit cost on a per-area basis, 
the power-specific cost was recalculated by considering full conversion efficiency and 
applying an installation factor of 1.2.  
For the cell configurations considered in this study, we assume equivalent stack capital 
costs per unit electrode area for both the CO 2 electroreduction coupled with water 
oxidation, and the CO 2 electroreduction coupled with hydrogen oxidation using a 
membrane separator, unless otherwise specified.

4) Sensitivity analysis 
Supplementary Information, Page 61 
A sensitivity analysis was conducted to evaluate the influence of key techno-economic 
parameters on the net present value (NPV) of the CO 2-to-formic acid electrolysis

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 58 -->

P
A
G
E
5
2
68 
 
process over a 20 -year operational period. The comparison between "better" and 
"worse" case scenarios for six critical variables revealed that stack cost and electricity 
price are the most influential factors affecting economic viability (Supplementary Fig. 
39, Supplementary Table 6). 
 
The NPV exhibited the greatest sensitivity to stack cost, ranging from 114.8 million 
USD in the better case to 96 million USD in the worse case. This result underscores the 
capital-intensive nature of the electrolyzer unit and highlights the need for cost 
reduction in stack materials, fabrication, and system assembly. Electricity price and 
CO2 cost similarly had strong effect, with NPV decreasing from 111.7 million USD to 
95.2 million USD, and 110.7 million USD to 96.2 million USD, respectively , 
confirming their dominant role in operating expenses, especially given the continuous 
power and feedstock demand of electrochemical CO2 conversion. Moderate sensitivity 
was observed for cell voltage, with NPVs varying from 105.7 to 101.2 million USD. 
This parameter influences energy consumption, making it important targets for system-
level optimization. The impact of selectivity toward formic acid was also evident, with 
NPV ranging from 106.8 to 99.3 million USD. Higher selectivity reduces undesired 
side reactions and improves carbon utilization efficiency, which in turn reduces both 
electricity demand and downstream separation cost. In contrast, current density showed 
the least impact on profitability, with NPV values of 103.3 and 103.6 million USD 
under better and worse conditions, respectively. This suggests that, within the studied 
range, the economic benefit of further increasing current density is limited once a 
baseline performance is achieved. 
 
In summary, the profitability of the CO 2-to-formic acid system is primarily driven by 
stack capital cost and electricity price. CO2 cost, system voltage, and product selectivity 
also influence economic outcomes to varying degrees, while current density plays a 
relatively minor role once operating thresholds are met. These findings provide a clear 
direction for cost-reduction strategies and technology development priorities in formic 
acid electrosynthesis.

5) Briefly mention in the main text 
Main text, Page 14 
 
A comprehensive TEA was carried out  (Supplementary Note 3 and Tables 5 -6). 
Assuming a CO2 feedstock cost of 40 USD ton−1 and a methanol feedstock cost of 470 
USD ton −1, and an electricity price of 0.03 USD kWh −1, the estimated levelized 
production cost of formic acid (HCOOH) is approximately 859 USD ton −1 (Fig. 5d). 
The major cost components contributing to this value include: separation cost, 
electrolyzer capital cost , and electricity expense. A pressurized extraction–distillation 
method was used for the ECR//HOR system, whereas electrodialysis followed by 
distillation was employed for the HER//AEM//MOR system. When the electricity price 
decreases to 0.02 USD kWh −1, the HCOOH production cost can be further reduced to 
830 USD ton −1 (Supplementary Fig. 39), which is comparable to the current market 
price of 830 USD ton−1, highlighting the economic viability of this integrated system.

2. Regarding Fig. 3, although the ECR/HOR configuration and the 2 mm gap 
significantly lowered the cell voltage, 1.7 V is still a relatively high cell voltage, 
especially if ECR (-0.2 V vs. SHE) and HOR (0 V vs. SHE) are paired. Can the authors 
break down the overpotential  losses? Also, with the ECR/OER and ECR/CEM/OER 
configurations, the cell voltages are between 5 and 6 V . Did the authors observe any 
degradation of the carbon paper substrate as carbon is prone to oxidation? For the 
ECR/HOR configuration without a membrane, why is the cell voltage so high (~6 V)? 
What is the origin of the resistance? 
 
Response:  
We thank the reviewer for the detailed and insightful comments. Below, we address 
each point separately. 
 
a) Regarding the ECR//HOR cell voltage (~1.7 V): 
We agree that the theoretical cell voltage between ECR (approximately −0.2 V vs. SHE) 
and HOR (0 V vs. SHE) should be close to 0.2 V under ideal conditions. However, the 
observed cell voltage of ~1.7 V mainly arises from the following overpotential factors:  
 
 Cathodic overpotential: Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst require s overpotentials of 1 V to 
achieve 100 mA cm −2 current densities in the three -electrode ECR//HOR 
electrolyzer with 10 mm electrolyte flow layer (Fig. R1), due to intrinsic reaction 
barriers and the hydrophobicity of the PTFE -coated GDE. When the electrolyte 
flow layer is reduced to 2 mm, the overpotential may be slightly lower due to 
decreased ionic resistance and improved mass transport. 
 
 
Fig. R1 | ECR LSV curves of Bi0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst in three-electrode ECR//HOR 
electrolyzer.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 61 -->

P
A
G
E
5
5
68 
 
 
 Anodic overpotential: Although HOR is thermodynamically favorable, in acidic 
environments, even Pt/C electrodes can exhibit kinetic limitations and hydrogen 
mass transport resistance under high current operation.  
 
 Ohmic losses: Despite the minimized electrode gap (2 mm), ohmic resistance 
(from electrolyte, contact, wiring, etc.) contributes significantly. As show n in 
Supplementary Fig. 21, EIS measurements indicate a solution resistance of ~4 Ω 
for the 2 mm cell, much lower than the ~27 Ω measured for the 10 mm cell, yet 
still non-negligible. 
 
 
Supplementary Fig. 21 | EIS spectra of the cell. a,  EIS spectra of the ECR//HOR 
electrolyzer with 2 mm and 10 mm interlayer. b, the enlargement of the a.  
 
 
b) Regarding the reviewer’s concern on carbon paper degradation at high voltages (5−6 
V) in ECR//OER and ECR//CEM//OER electrolyzers: we did observe signs of carbon 
oxidation and mechanical fragility after long -term electrolysis at such high voltages. 
This highlights the limitation of using carbon -based substrates in oxidative conditions 
and underscores the need for robust anode supports in such systems. Therefore, we used 
Pt foil to serve as the anode during the tests in the ECR //OER and ECR//CEM//OER 
electrolyzers. 
 
c) We thank the reviewer for raising this question regarding the seemingly high cell 
voltage (~6 V) in the membrane -free ECR//HOR configuration. However, we believe 
there may be a misunderstanding.  In our manuscript, the reported cell voltage for the 
membrane-free ECR//HOR configuration is approximately 3.0 V , not 6.0 V . The higher 
voltage (~5.2 V) corresponds to the ECR//CE M//HOR configuration, where a cation 
exchange membrane (CEM) is used between the electrodes.  The elevated cell voltage 
in the membrane-containing system arises primarily from the ohmic resistance of the

membrane, which limits ion transport.  
 
 
3. How scalable is the membraneless electrolyzer design? It seems that all results for 
the CO 2 electrolyzer were obtained using very small (1 cm^2) electrodes. Did the 
authors attempt any measurements at a larger scale to see how/if performance changes? 
 
Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer ’s valuable comment regarding the scalability of our 
membraneless electrolyzer design. To address this concern, we conducted additional 
experiments using a larger electrode with a geometric area of 2 cm × 2 cm under the 
current density of 100 mA cm−2. Specifically, to further suppress carbonate precipitation, 
the CO2 feed gas was first humidified by passing it through a glass bubbler containing 
0.2 M HCl solution before introduction into the cathode chamber of the flow cell 
(Science 2025, 388, 6752, eadr3834). The presence of volatile acids can facilitate the 
conversion of carbonates into more soluble potassium salts.  The CO2 flow rate was 
controlled at 30 sccm throughout the test.  The scaled -up 2 cm × 2 cm electrolyzer 
achieved stable operation for approximately 48 hours with a comparable HCOOH 
Faradaic efficiency of around 90% (Supplementary Fig. 24), confirming the robustness 
and scalability of the acidic membrane-free configuration for extended operation.

Main text, Page 19 
For the long -term stability tests of the ECR//HOR electrolyzer, electrochemical 
measurements were performed in a two -electrode configuration using flow cells with 
electrode areas of either 0.5 cm × 2 cm or 2 cm × 2 cm. The corresponding electrolyte 
flow layer thicknesses were 2 mm and 3 mm, respectively. Electrolyte volumes of 
500 mL (1 cm2 cell) and 1000 mL (4 cm 2 cell) were circulated at flow rates of 8 mL 
min−1 and 5 mL min−1, respectively, and refreshed every 24 hours to maintain stability. 
In both setups, the CO2 feed was humidified by bubbling through HCl solutions (0.05 M 
for the 1 cm 2 cell and 0. 2 M for the 4 cm 2 cell) prior to entering the cathode 
compartment8. CO2 flow rates were set at 20 sccm and 30 sccm for the smaller and 
larger cells, respectively. 
 
 
4. How stable can the electrolyzer system be beyond 50 hours, which is a relatively 
short time for a durability test for electrolyzer technology? 
 
Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer’s insightful comment regarding the long -term operational 
stability of our membrane -free CO2 reduction system. We conducted additional 
electrolysis experiments under optimized conditions to directly address this concern. 
Specifically, to further suppress carbonate precipitation, the CO2 feed gas was first 
humidified by passing it through a glass bubbler containing 0.05 M HCl solution before 
introduction into the cathode chamber of the flow cell  (Science 2025, 388, 6752, 
eadr3834). The presence of volatile acids can facilitate the conversion of carbonates

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 64 -->

P
A
G
E
5
8
68 
 
into more soluble potassium salts.  The CO2 flow rate was contro lled at 20 sccm 
throughout the test. 
 
With the optimized conditions, the membrane-free electrolyzer with an electrode area 
of 2 cm × 0.5 cm exhibited stable operation for over 208 hours, maintaining a Faradaic 
efficiency for HCOOH above 90% (Fig. 4f). This greatly surpasses previously reported 
stability durations (~50 hours), indicating that carbonate-induced pore blockage can be 
effectively suppressed. Furthermore, the scaled -up 2 cm × 2 cm electrolyzer also 
achieved stable operation for approximately 48 h ours with a comparable HCOOH 
Faradaic efficiency of around 90% ( Supplementary Fig. 25), confirming the 
robustness and scalability of the acidic membrane -free configuration for extended 
operation. 
 
To further substantiate these findings, we have compared ou r long-term stability data 
with representative state-of-the-art CO2 reduction systems reported in the literature (see 
Supplementary Table 4). Our system achieves competitive or superior performance in 
terms of both operational duration and product selectivity. 
 
The new experimental data have been added as Supplementary Fig. 25 in the revised 
manuscript, and the discussion has been updated accordingly. We believe these 
additional results provide strong evidence that our design is technically feasible for 
prolonged CO2 electroreduction without significant carbonate -induced transport 
limitations.

For the long -term stability tests of the ECR//HOR electrolyzer, electrochemical 
measurements were performed in a two -electrode configuration using flow cells with 
electrode areas of either 0.5 cm × 2 cm or 2 cm × 2 cm. The corresponding electrolyte

5. Since CO was observed as shown in Fig. 2, did the authors observe any CO poisoning 
effect with the Pt anode? If so, how significant is it, especially during long -term

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 66 -->

P
A
G
E
6
0
68 
 
operation? 
  
Response: 
 
We thank the reviewer for raising this important point about possible CO crossover and 
Pt anode poisoning. In our system, the cathodically generated CO is confined to the 
cathode gas−liquid interface and does not diffuse through the bulk electrolyte to reach 
the anode side, given the very low solubility of CO in aqueous media and the proton -
dominated transport direction. We have performed additional experiments t o further 
rule out any CO poisoning effect on the Pt anode. 
 
1) CO stripping tests 
To directly verify whether CO produced at the ECR cathode during long-term operation 
could diffuse to the anode and cause CO poisoning, CO stripping experiments and 
cyclic voltammetry were performed on the commercial Pt/C -based gas diffusion 
electrode after stability tests. The presence or absence of a distinct CO oxidation peak 
was used to evaluate possible CO adsorption on the Pt surface. 
 
After the stability test in the flow cell, the Pt/C GDE was carefully transferred to a 
three-electrode cell using 0.5 M K2SO4 + H2SO4 (pH ~1.8) as the electrolyte. A graphite 
rod was used as the counter electrode and a saturated calomel electrode served as the 
reference. Cyclic voltammetry scans were first performed directly as shown in 
Supplementary Fig. 28. No CO oxidation peak was observed, indicating that no 
significant CO adsorption occurred on the Pt/C anode during long -term operation . 
Subsequently, to confirm the electrode’s activity for CO oxidation, high-purity CO gas 
was bubbled through the electrolyte for 20 minutes while holding to 0.05 V vs. RHE to 
ensure sufficient CO adsorption on catalyst surface . The CO stripping test was then 
conducted by CV from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at a scan rate of 20 mV s −1. A clear 
CO oxidation peak appeared at approximately 0.88 V during the first CO stripping scan 
and disappeared in the second scan, confirming that the Pt surface remained fully active 
for CO oxidation and was not poisoned by CO generated at the cathode during long-
term operation1-3. These results demonstrate that the CO generated at the cathode did 
not crossover to the anode to cause CO poisoning under the tested flow cell conditions. 
These combined results demonstrate that the Pt anode was not affected by CO poisoning 
under our operating conditions.

We conducted CO stripping experiments on the commercial Pt/C gas diffusion 
electrode (GDE) after the long-term stability test. After operation in the flow cell, the 
Pt/C GDE was carefully transferred to a conventional three -electrode cell using 0.5 M 
K2SO4 + H2SO4 (pH ~1.8) as the electrolyte, with a saturated calomel electrode as the 
reference electrode and a graphite rod as the counter electrode. Cyclic voltammetry (CV) 
was first performed by scanning from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at 20 mV s −1 to detect 
any CO oxidation peak. Subsequently, high -purity CO gas was bubbled through the 
electrolyte for 20 min while holding the working electrode potential at 0.05 V vs. RHE 
to ensure sufficient CO adsorption. The cell was then flushed with fresh N 2-purged 
electrolyte for 10 min to remove residual CO. Finally, CO stripping was carried out by 
CV scanning twice from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at a scan rate of 20 mV s−1. 
 
2) Long-term stability test 
During a continuous 208 -hour operation under the current density of 100 mA cm−2 in 
ECR//HOR electrolyzer with 1  cm2 electrode area , the system exhibited stable 
performance, maintaining a HCOOH faradaic efficiency of approximately 90% 
throughout the entire duration  (Fig. 4 f). These results indicate that any possible 
hydrogen crossover has negligible influence on the cathodic CO2 reduction reaction or 
the overall product selectivity.

REVIEWER COMMENTS 
Reviewer #1 
 
I have carefully reviewed the revised manuscript and the authors ’ point-by-point responses to my 
initial comments. The authors have provided additional experiments, analyses, and discussions that 
partially address my concerns, including performance comparisons with membrane-based systems, 
qualitative discussion of ηH+ transport, and mechanistic insights into the Bi –Ag catalyst. These 
revisions improve the  clarity and completeness of the work. However, several of my original 
concerns remain only partially resolved. 
 
Response: 
Thanks for your valuable suggestions, which have significantly improved our work. Following your 
advice, we incorporated additional C OMSOL simulations to examine the effects of electrolyte 
composition and membrane properties on proton transport and their impact on 𝜂𝐻+. For the catalytic 
mechanism, we employed in situ attenuated total reflection surface -enhanced infrared absorption 
spectroscopy (ATR-SEIRAS) to probe intermediate binding and used XPS to investigate electron 
transfer on Bi. We also expanded the discussion to provide deeper mechanistic insights into the Bi-
Ag catalyst. Regarding the TEA, we revised the analysis using As pen-based process simulations 
that account for H2 supply, byproduct management, and industrial-scale operation, enabling a more 
rigorous and comprehensive evaluation. 
 
1. While the Nernst –Planck equation is now introduced, no quantitative modeling or param eter 
measurement is provided. Please consider simplified transport simulations (e.g., COMSOL) to 
quantify the effects of membrane thickness, ion selectivity, and electrolyte composition on ηH+. 
 
Response: 
 
Thanks for your suggestion. We performed COMSOL si mulations to quantify the effects of 
membrane thickness, proton selectivity, and electrolyte composition on proton flux and 𝜂𝐻+. The 
model was constructed as a two-dimensional homogeneous system consisting of a catholyte//Nafion 
membrane//anolyte geometry (Supplementary Fig. 2). Mobile ions included H+, K+, and SO42−. The 
Nafion membrane carried a fixed negative charge (cSO3 = 1 mol/L), and ion selectivity was described 
by an effective H+ transport number (𝛼𝐻+). The baseline parameters were an electrolyte composition 
of 0.5 M K2SO4 + 0.01 M H2SO4, a membrane thickness of 200 μm, and a proton selectivity of 0.8. 
Individual parameters were then varied to assess their specific effects.  Relevant discussions have 
been added to the revised manuscript, also copied below.  
 
Main text, Page 6 
We then employed COMSOL to further analyze the influence of membrane and electrolyte 
composition to proton transport (Supplementary Fig. 2 and Methods). We firstly investiga ted the 
influence of membrane thickness (Supplementary Fig. 3), a thicker membrane restricted proton 
transfer, resulting in a non -uniform proton flux and hindering transport from the anode to the

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 76 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
cathode (right to left). Consequently, the proton concentration distribution across the cell was non-
uniform, and the 𝜂𝐻+ at the cathode side was low. In contrast, in the absence of a membrane (bottom 
panel), proton flux was faster and more uniform from the anode to the cathode, leading to a uniform 
proton concentration distribution and a significantly higher 𝜂𝐻+ near the cathode compared with the 
system with membrane. In addition, higher proton selectivity could also promote proton transport 
from the anode to the cathode (Supplementary Fig. 4). As proton selectivity decreased, H+ flux, H+ 
concentration, and  𝜂𝐻+ at the cathode side all declined, indicating that a membrane with high proton 
selectivity is more effective at maintaining catholyte pH.  We also investigate the influence of 
Electrolyte composition (Supplementary Fig. 5).  As H + concentration increases, proton transport 
from the anode to the cathode becomes more difficult. When both the anode and cathode have high 
proton concentrations, reduced chemical potential, strong ionic shielding, and near -saturation of 
proton-conducting channels in the membrane collectively limit the effective proton migration rate. 
Taken together, a thinner membrane with high proton selectivity and an electrolyte with low proton 
concentration are more favorable for proton transport.

To better explore the effect of a single variable on mass transfer behavior, there are a constant H -
ion concentration (0 mol/L) and a constant potential (0 V) at the cathode -catholyte interface, and 
there are a constant current density (I = 100 mA cm −2) and a constant potential (1V) at the anode -
anolyte interface. Mobile ions included H +, K+, and SO 42−. The Nafion membrane carried a fixed 
negative charge (c SO3 = 1 mol/L), and ion selectivity was described by an effective H+ transport 
number (𝛼𝐻+). The baseline parameters were an electrolyte composition of 0.5 M K2SO4 + 0.01 M 
H2SO4, a membrane thickness of 200 μm, and a proton selectivity of 0.8. Individual parameters 
were then varied to assess their specific effects. 
All finite -element simulations were conducted in COMSOL Multiphysics. Unless otherwise 
specified, all other parameter conditions were set to be consistent with the electrochemical 
measurement conditions as shown in Supplementary Table 1.

2. The SHINERS data suggest a lower onset potential for *CO2- formation on Bi0.5–Ag0.5, but the 
mechanistic explanation remains focused on conductivity. Please elaborate on whether Ag modifies 
the adsorption energies of *CO2- or *OCHO, or alters the electronic structure of Bi. 
 
Response: 
Thanks for your suggestion. To further clarify the catalytic mechanism, we have added additiona l 
in-situ and ex-situ characterizations to probe the catalyst structure and reaction intermediates, 
including XPS after CO 2 reduction and in -situ ATR-SEIRAS. We also reanalyzed the data and 
expanded the discussion to provide a more comprehensive explanation of the reaction mechanism. 
Please see the revised main text, also copied below.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 79 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
 
Main text, Page 8 
After ECR, we disassembled the electrolyzer and characterized the catalysts post -reaction. The 
morphology of Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 was well preserved  (Supplementary Fig. 15) . XPS analysis revealed 
electron transfer between Bi and Ag: the Bi 4f core levels exhibited a positive binding-energy shift 
upon introducing Ag  (Supplementary Fig. 16) , indicating electron withdrawal from Bi. This 
electronic structure change is further supported by the product distribution: although Ag typically 
favors CO formation over formate, only trace CO was detected in our system, suggesting that the 
electronic structures of both Bi and Ag were altered.  To probe the reaction intermediates, in-situ 
attenuated total reflection surface -enhanced infrared absorption spectroscopy (ATR-SEIRAS) was 
performed (Supplementary Fig. 17). A characteristic band at approximately 1400 cm−1 was observed 
on both Bi and Bi 0.5-Ag0.5, commonly assigned to *OCHO intermediates adsorbed on the catalyst 
surface1. This is consistent with previous reports that formate is generated through hydrogenation 
of the C atom in *OCHO2. As the potential shifted negatively, the *OCHO signal intensity gradually 
increased, indicating higher surface coverage of this intermediate. Notably, Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 exhibited a 
pronounced increase in the intensity of a band near 1250 cm −1 with increasing overpotential, 
attributed to the asymmetric −COO− stretching vibration of activated *CO 2− species3. In contrast, 
pure Bi showed a much weaker increase, suggesting that Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 is more favorable for CO 2 
adsorption and activation. In-situ shell isolated nanoparticle -enhanced Raman spectroscopy 
(SHINERS) measurements (Supplementary Fig. 18) further confirmed that both Bi and Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 
follow similar *CO 2− pathways toward formate formation 4,5, but Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 required a lower 
overpotential for *CO 2− formation. These results indicate that the improved CO 2-to-formate 
conversion performance of Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 arises from both electronic effects that promote CO 2 
adsorption/activation and the enhanced charge -transfer proces s enabled by the high electrical 
conductivity of Ag. The ECR performance of the Bi 0.5-Ag0.5 catalyst was also assessed in neutral 
and alkaline electrolytes, where formate Faradaic efficiencies consistently exceeded 90% 
(Supplementary Fig. 19). Note that the electrolysis time was controlled to 10 min to exclude the 
possible effects of salt precipitation.

The experimental details are included in the manuscript on Page 18: 
For the in -situ ATR -SEIRAS ( Attenuated total reflectance -surface-enhanced infrared 
absorption spectroscopy) experiments, Bi0.5-Ag0.5 and Bi catalysts deposited on Au-coated Si 
prisms were employed as the working electrodes, while an Ag/AgCl electrode and a Pt foil 
served as the reference and counter electrodes, respectively. Potentiodynamic ATR -SEIRAS 
spectra were recorded in 0.5 M KHCO 3 electrolyte saturated with C O2. During the entire 
measurement, CO 2 was continuously purged into the electrochemical cell at 20 sccm to 
maintain electrolyte saturation and enhance mass transport. Infrared signals were collected 
using a Thermo Fisher IS50 spectrometer equipped with an MCT-A detector, with unpolarized 
IR radiation incident at 60° and a spectral resolution of 8 cm −1. The spectra are presented in 
absorbance mode as −log(I/I0), where I and I 0 denote the intensities of the reflected radiation 
from the sample and the reference, respectively.

3. The proposed MOR –HER coupling is presented as a proof -of-concept, but lacks quantitative 
energy and cost analysis. Please compare the energy efficiency of external H2 supply versus internal 
H₂ generation (including MOR energy demand and CO2 byproduct ma nagement), and provide a 
preliminary TEA for industrial-scale operation. 
 
Response: 
Thanks for your suggestion. We have revised the techno -economic analysis using Aspen -based 
process simulations that consider H 2 supply (external and internal H 2 supply), by product 
management, and industrial-scale operation. The associated energy and material costs have been 
incorporated into the TEA section, providing a more rigorous and comprehensive evaluation. Please 
refer to Supplementary Note 2, which is also provided below. 
 
Supplementary Note. 2 | Techno-Economic Assessment 
We conducted techno -economic assessment (TEA) for various electrolyzer systems presented in 
this work under a production goal of 90 kmol HCOOH/hr and an annual operating time of 8000 
hours (i.e., 9 0 tonnes/day) (Supplementary Table 6). The lifespan of the plant is set to 20 years 6. 
Capital expenditure (CAPEX) and operating expense (OPEX) are both presented in the form of 
levelized production cost of HCOOH (denoted as LC, with a unit of USD/tonne HCOOH). Aspen 
Plus was utilized to establish material balance and provide necessary data to support calculations in 
TEA. Gas mixture containing unreacted CO 2 is recovered using pressure swing adsorpting (PSA) 
while extractive distillation is employed to obtain 95  wt% HCOOH product. The water recovered 
from ex tractive distillation was reused to sustain electrolyte concentration, reducing the cost of 
water. 
1. ECR//HOR and ECR//CEM//HOR configurations 
In the system based on HOR, the main reaction to produce HCOOH in these two configurations is 
given as follows: 
 Overall: CO2 + H2 → HCOOH 
 Cathode: CO2 + 2H+ + 2e− → HCOOH 
 Anode:H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We also considered two side reactions 
observed in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1 
and 𝑄𝑠2, respectively: 
 Overall: H2 →  H2 
 Cathode: 2H+ + 2e− → H2 
 Anode: H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
 
 Overall: CO2 + H2 → CO + H2O 
 Cathode: CO2 + 2H+ + 2e− → CO + H2O 
 Anode: H2 − 2e− → 2H+

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 83 -->

The process flow diagram for ECR//HOR configuration developed in Aspen Plus is shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 44. Specifically, electrolyte containing 0.5 M K2SO4 and 0.005 M H2SO4 is fed 
to the electrolyzer while H 2 and CO2 are fed to the anode side and cathode side, respectively. The 
unreacted H2 coming out from the anode side is recycled and th e gas mixture coming out from the 
cathode side goes through pressure swing adsorption to recovery unreacted CO2. The liquid mixture 
containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis to reduce the water amount for 
efficient extractive distillation. For ECR//HOR, a ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 90%: 8%: 2% was used 
when establishing material balance. 
Similarly, the process flow diagram for ECR//CEM//HOR configuration developed in Aspen Plus 
is shown on Supplementary Fig. 45.  For ECR//CEM//HOR, a rat io of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 =
52%: 46%: 2% was used when establishing material balance.

(4) Electrodialysis process for concentrating formic acid 
To improve the separation efficiency and reduce energy consumption, the liquid mixture coming 
out from the electrolyzer is first concentrated via electrodialysis to increase the HCOOH 
concentration from 0.1 M to 2 M. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin 9, the CAPEX and 
OPEX associated with the electrodialysis are estimated at 0.06 USD kg −1 and 0.13 USD kg −1, 
respectively. For a fixed production goal of 90 kmol/hr HCOOH, aspen simulations indicated a mass 
flowrate of 4142.31 kg/hr.

(5) Cathode Gas Separation 
Pressure swing adsorption (PSA) is a mature industrial technology that offers an efficient, cost -
effective, and space-saving approach for gas separation10. In CO2 electrolysis processes, the reactor 
exhaust typically contains a mixture of unreacted CO2, target gas products, and by-product hydrogen. 
A comparable industrial scenario is biogas upgrading, where raw biogas composed mainly of 
methane and CO 2 is purified using PSA systems. Insights from commercial biogas upgrading 
projects have been used to estimate the capital and operational requirements for PSA units in CO 2 
electrolysis applications. In line with these industrial benchmarks, we adopt a reference capital cost 
of 1,989,043 USD per 1,000 m 3/h of throughput, with a scaling factor of 0.7 and a capacity factor 
of 0.9 to account for economies of scale and operation flexibility11. The primary operating expense 
for PSA is electricity consumption, estimated at 0.25 kWh per cubic meter of gas treated 11. 
Additional costs related to gas compression and storage downstream of the separation unit are 
assumed negligible in this analysis. The volume of gas mixture coming out from cathode is obtained 
via Aspen Plus simulations. The volume of gas mixture under ECR//HOR configuration is 1000.12 
m3/h while the volume of gas mixture under ECR//CEM//HOR configuration is 2803.87 m 3/h. We 
assume a lifespan of 20 years for the PSA unit and a discount rate of 7%.

(6) Liquid Production Separation 
For the liquid product separation, an extractio n-distillation approach was adopted based on a 
previously reported process design12. In this approach, the 2 M HCOOH first processed through an 
extraction distillation column (EDC), followed by entrainer recover column (ERC), as shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 46. Sulfolane was selected as the entrainer for H2O/HCOOH maximum-boiling 
azeotropic system. The sulfolane price is set to 2.2 USD/kg (ref.13). Rigorous extraction-distillation 
simualtions were performed in Aspen Plus, and it is shown that formic acid can be concentrated to 
over 98 wt% via this two -step separation, meeting commercial purity standards. The process 
parameters of the two columns, including reflux ratio, feed location, number of plates, operating 
pressure, were consistent with the literature 6. We assume a lifespan of 20 years for the extraction-
distillation unit and a discount rate of 7%. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 46 |  Process flow diagram of the extraction -distillation separation of 
formic acid. The 2 M HCOOH solution is first processed through an extraction distillation column 
(EDC) and subsequently through an entrainer recovery column (ERC) for solvent regeneration and 
HCOOH purification.

For both ECR//HOR and ECR//CEM//HOR configurations, electrolyte including K2SO4 and H2SO4

2. ECR//OER and ECR//CEM//OER configurations 
In the system based on OER, the main reaction to produce HCOOH in these two configurations is 
given as follows: 
 Overall: 2CO2 + 2H2O(l) → 2HCOOH + O2  
 Cathode: 2CO2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2HCOOH 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We also considered two side reactions 
observed in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1 
and 𝑄𝑠2, respectively: 
 Overall: 2H2O →  2H2 + O2   
 Cathode: 4H+ + 4e− → 2H2 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
 
 Overall: 2CO2 → 2CO + O2 
 Cathode: 2CO2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2CO + 2H2O 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
The process flow diagram for ECR//OER configuration developed in Aspen Plus is shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 47. Specifically, electrolyte containing 0.5 M K2SO4 and 0.005 M H2SO4 is fed 
to the electrolyzer while CO2 is fed to the cathode side. The gas mixture coming out from the cathode 
side goes through pressure swing adsorption to recovery unreacted CO 2. The liquid mixture 
containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis to reduce the water amount for 
efficient extractive distillation. To account for the oxidation of HCOOH at  anode, a ratio of 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 54%: 8%: 2% was used when establishing material balance for ECR//OER. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 47 | Process flow diagram of ECR//OER. 
 
Similarly, the process flow diagram for ECR//CEM//OER configuration developed in Aspen Plus is

was used when establishing material balance.

3. ECR//HOR coupled with HER//AEM//MOR configuration 
The main reaction to produce HCOO− in HER//AEM//MOR is given as follows: 
 Overall: CH3OH + OH− → HCOO−+ 2H2 
 Cathode: 4H2O + 4e− → 2H2 + 4OH−  
 Anode: CH3OH + 5OH− − 4e− → HCOO− + 4H2O 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We considered a side reaction observed 
in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1: 
 Overall: CH3OH + H2O → CO2 + 3H2 
 Cathode: 6H2O + 6e− → 3H2 + 6OH−

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 94 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
 Anode: CH3OH + 6OH− − 6e− → CO2 + 5H2O 
The process flow diagram for ECR//HOR coupled with HER//AEM//MOR configuration developed 
in Aspen Plus is shown on Supplementary Fig. 49. For the HER//AEM//MOR configuration, 0.1 M 
KOH is used as electrolyte on both anode and cathode side. The generated H2 at cathode side is fed 
to the anode side of ECR//HOR, eliminating the need of buying fresh H 2 from market. In addition, 
the pure CO2 generated from the anode side of HER//AEM//MOR will be sent to the cathode side 
of ECR//HOR to reduce CO 2 consumption. The  HCOO−  generated at the anode side of 
HER//AEM//MOR is 0.2  M and will undergo electrodialysis to obtain HCOOH, as well as 
increasing its concentration to 2 M for efficient liquid separation. A ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1 = 90%: 10% 
was used when establishing material balance for HER//AEM//MOR while a list of ratios including 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 100%: 0%: 0%, 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 90%: 8%: 2%, 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 80%: 18%: 2%, 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 70%: 28%: 2% were used when establishing material balance for ECR//H OR to 
investigate the influence of faradiac efficiency on the TEA performance of the coupled 
configuration. As the total HCOOH amount obtained from the coupled configuration is fixed at 90 
kmol/hr, the HCOOH obtained from the ECR//HOR and HER//AEM//MOR und er the four 
scenarios are 62069 + 27931 mol/hr, 60000 + 30000 mol/hr, 57600 + 32400 mol/hr, and 54782.6 + 
35217.4 mol/hr, respectively. In the following calculations, we use the second scenario as example 
to demonstrate the calculation procedures.

(8) Electrolyte Cost 
For ECR //HOR configuration, electrolyte including K 2SO4 and H 2SO4 is neither consumed or 
generated during the entire production cycle. For HER//AEM//MOR configuration, the OH − 
generated at the cathode side via the main reaction is transported to the anode side. T he flowrates 
of electrolyte required to sustain the desired concentrations (i.e., 1M K + and 0.01M H + for 
ECR//HOR and 1M OH− for HER//AEM//MOR) are obtained from aspen simulations, and they are 
shown in Supplementary Table 9.

(ref.17), respectively. Then the levelized cost of electrolyte throughout th e plant ’s lifespan are

1. By reviewing the point -by-point response to the reviewers, the reviewer believes that the 
authors' citation and methodology regarding TEA still have significant flaws. For example, in the 
response to comment 1, Reference 7 does not involve the calculation of TEA, so the reviewer could 
not verify the accuracy of the authors' citation. (All process parameters and energy consumption 
estimates were re ferenced from the literature7. Reference 7. Du, Z.Y. et al. Promoting Water 
Activation via Molecular Engineering Enables Efficient Asymmetric C -C Coupling during CO2 
Electroreduction. J. Am. Chem. Soc. 146, 32870-32879 (2024).)  
 
Similarly, in Comment 3, I  could not find the corresponding description —Reference 21 does not 
include an author named Mahinder Ramdin. (According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin21. 21. 
Bazmi, M., Gong, J., Jessen, K. & Tsotsis, T. Waste CO2 capture and utilization for methanol 
production via a novel membrane contactor reactor process: Techno-economic analysis (TEA), and 
comparison with other existing and emerging technologies. Chem. Eng. Process.: Process Intensif. 
201 (2024).） 
 
Therefore, regarding these two issues about TEA, I believe the authors have not addressed my 
concerns because I cannot assess the accuracy of their response. The data sources provided by the 
authors are unreliable, so I do not recommend accepting this article for publication in this journal. 
 
Response:  
We sincerely apologize for the mistakes related to reference in our response letter and greatly 
appreciate the reviewers ’ careful review. When preparing our response to the reviewers ’ 
comments, we mistakenly copied the text (w/o endnote link) from the Supple mentary Information 
into the response letter as plain text. As a result, the references number (ref. 7 and ref. 21) in the 
response letter were not updated. Furthermore, since we later revised other references in the SI, the 
numbering of certain references (ref. 7 and ref. 21) in the response letter became inconsistent with 
both the SI (ref. 10 and ref. 24) and the response letter’s reference list. However, the references in 
the SI are entirely correct. Please refer to the screenshot below for details. We s incerely apologize 
for this mistake.  We have revised the  techno-economic analysis using Aspen -based process 
simulations, enabling a more rigorous and comprehensive evaluation.  Please check the 
Supplementary Note 2 for details, also copied below.

Main text, Page 16 
A comprehensive TEA was carried out  (Supplementary Note  2, Supplementary Figs. 44 -50). 
Assuming a CO2 feedstock cost of 40 USD ton−1 and a methanol feedstock cost of 470 USD ton−1, 
and an electricity price of 0.03 USD kWh −1, integration with a separation process involving 
electrodialysis followed by extraction–distillation yields an estimated levelized production cost of 
formic acid of approximately 729.8 USD ton −1 (Fig. 5d). The major cost components contributing 
to this value include: separation cost, electrolyzer capital cost , and electricity expense. Although 
this value is slightly higher than that of direct H 2 supply (Supplementary Note 4) , it reduces the 
costs associated with H2 transport and storage (factors not considered in our TEA) and also enhances 
operational safety. This value remains below the current market price 20 (830 USD ton −1), and the 
overall cost can be further reduced as the methanol price and electricity price decreases 
(Supplementary Fig. 50). 
 
Supplementary Note. 2 | Techno-Economic Assessment 
We conducted techno-economic assessment (TEA) for various electrolyzer systems presented in 
this work under a production goal of 90 kmol HCOOH/hr and an annual operating time of 8000 
hours (i.e., 90 tonnes/day)  (Supplementary Table 6). The lifespan of the plant is set  to 20 years 6. 
Capital expenditure (CAPEX) and operating expense (OPEX) are both presented in the form of 
levelized production cost of HCOOH (denote d as LC, with a unit of USD/tonne HCOOH). Aspen 
Plus was utilized to establish material balance and provide necessary data to support calculations in 
TEA. Gas mixture containing unreacted CO 2 is recovered using pressure swing adsorpting (PSA) 
while extractive distillation is employed to obtain 95  wt% HCOOH product. The water recovered 
from extractive distillation was reused to sustain electrolyte concentration, reducing the cost of 
water. 
1. ECR//HOR and ECR//CEM//HOR configurations 
In the system based on HOR, the main reaction to produce HCOOH in these two configurations is 
given as follows: 
 Overall: CO2 + H2 → HCOOH 
 Cathode: CO2 + 2H+ + 2e− → HCOOH

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 105 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
 Anode:H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We also considered two side reactions 
observed in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1 
and 𝑄𝑠2, respectively: 
 Overall: H2 →  H2 
 Cathode: 2H+ + 2e− → H2 
 Anode: H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
 
 Overall: CO2 + H2 → CO + H2O 
 Cathode: CO2 + 2H+ + 2e− → CO + H2O 
 Anode: H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
The process flow diagram for ECR//HOR configuration developed in Aspen Plus is shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 44. Specifically, electrolyte containing 0.5 M K2SO4 and 0.005 M H2SO4 is fed 
to the electrolyzer while H 2 and CO2 are fed to the anode side and cathode side, respectively. The 
unreacted H2 coming out from the anode side is recycled and the gas mixture coming out from the 
cathode side goes through pressure swing adsorption to recovery unreacted CO2. The liquid mixture 
containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis to reduce the water amount for 
efficient extractive distillation. For ECR//HOR, a ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 90%: 8%: 2% was used 
when establishing material balance. 
Similarly, the process flow diagram for ECR//CEM//HOR configuration developed in Aspen Plus 
is shown on Supplementary Fig. 45.  For ECR//CEM//HOR, a ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 =
52%: 46%: 2% was used when establishing material balance.

(4) Electrodialysis process for concentrating formic acid 
To improve the separation efficiency and reduce energy consumption, the liquid mixture coming 
out from the electrolyzer is first concentrated via electrodialysis to increase the HCOOH 
concentration from 0.1 M to 2 M. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin 9, the CAPEX and 
OPEX associated with the electrodialysis are estimated at 0.06 USD kg −1 and 0.13 USD kg −1, 
respectively. For a fixed production goal of 90 kmol/hr HCOOH, aspen simulations indicated a mass 
flowrate of 4142.31 kg/hr.

(5) Cathode Gas Separation 
Pressure swing adsorption (PSA) is a mature industrial technology that offers an efficient, cost -
effective, and space-saving approach for gas separation10. In CO2 electrolysis processes, the reactor 
exhaust typically contains a mixture of unreacted CO2, target gas products, and by-product hydrogen. 
A comparable industrial scenario is biogas upgrading, where raw biogas composed mainly of 
methane and CO 2 is purified using PSA  systems. Insights from commercial biogas upgrading 
projects have been used to estimate the capital and operational requirements for PSA units in CO 2 
electrolysis applications. In line with these industrial benchmarks, we adopt a reference capital cost 
of 1,989,043 USD per 1,000 m 3/h of throughput, with a scaling factor of 0.7 and a capacity factor 
of 0.9 to account for economies of scale and operation flexibility11. The primary operating expense 
for PSA is electricity consumption, estimated at 0.25 kWh per cubic meter of gas treated 11. 
Additional costs related to gas compression and storage downstream of the separation unit are 
assumed negligible in this analysis. The volume of gas mixture coming out from cathode is obtained 
via Aspen Plus simulations. The volume of gas mixture under ECR//HOR configuration is 1000.12 
m3/h while the volume of gas mixture under ECR//CEM//HOR configuration is 2803.87 m 3/h. We 
assume a lifespan of  20 years for the PSA unit and a discount rate of 7%.

(6) Liquid Production Separation 
For the liquid product separat ion, an extraction -distillation approach was adopted based on a 
previously reported process design12. In this approach, the 2 M HCOOH first processed through an 
extraction distillation column (EDC), followed by entrainer recover column (ERC), as shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 46. Sulfolane was selected as the entrainer for H2O/HCOOH maximum-boiling 
azeotropic system. The sulfolane price is set to 2.2 USD/kg (ref.13). Rigorous extraction-distillation

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 109 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
simualtions were performed in Aspen Plus, and it is shown that formic acid can be concentrated to 
over 98 wt% via this two -step separation, meeting commercial purity standards. The process 
parameters of the two columns, including reflux ratio, feed location, number of plates, operating 
pressure, were consistent with the literature 6. We assume a lifespan of 20 years for the extraction-
distillation unit and a discount rate of 7%. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 46 |  Process flow diagram of the extraction -distillation separation of 
formic acid. The 2 M HCOOH solution is first processed through an extraction distillation column 
(EDC) and subsequently through an entrainer recovery column (ERC) for solvent regeneration and 
HCOOH purification.

2. ECR//OER and ECR//CEM//OER configurations 
In the system based on OER, the main reaction to produce HCOOH in these two configurations is 
given as follows: 
 Overall: 2CO2 + 2H2O(l) → 2HCOOH + O2  
 Cathode: 2CO2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2HCOOH 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We also considered two side reactions 
observed in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1 
and 𝑄𝑠2, respectively: 
 Overall: 2H2O →  2H2 + O2   
 Cathode: 4H+ + 4e− → 2H2 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
 
 Overall: 2CO2 → 2CO + O2

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 112 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
 Cathode: 2CO2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2CO + 2H2O 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
The process flow diagram for ECR//OER configuration developed in Aspen Plus is shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 47. Specifically, electrolyte containing 0.5 M K2SO4 and 0.005 M H2SO4 is fed 
to the electrolyzer while CO2 is fed to the cathode side. The gas mixture coming out from the cathode 
side goes through pressure swing adsorption to recovery unreacted CO 2. The liquid mixture 
containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis to reduce the water amount for 
efficient extractive distillation. To account for the oxidation of HCOOH at  anode, a ratio of 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 54%: 8%: 2% was used when establishing material balance for ECR//OER. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 47 | Process flow diagram of ECR//OER. 
 
Similarly, the process flow diagram for ECR//CEM//OER configuration developed in Aspen Plus is 
shown on Supplementary Fig. 48. For ECR//CEM//HOR, a ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 75%: 23%: 2% 
was used when establishing material balance. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 48 | Process flow diagram of ECR//CEM//OER.

(8) Electrolyte Cost 
For ECR//OER configuration, electrolyte including K 2SO4 and H 2SO4 is neither consumed or 
generated during the entire production cycle. For ECR//CEM//OER configuration, we assume there 
is no transportation of K + across the membrane during the production to facilitate the modeling 
process. The flowrates of K 2SO4 and H 2SO4 required to  sustain the desired electrolyte 
concentrations (i.e., 1 M K + and 0.01 M H +) are obtained from aspen simulations, as shown in 
Supplementary Table 8. 
 
We assume a 48 hr stock of electrolyte to account for production fluctuations, and the prices for 
K2SO4 and H2SO4 are set to 0.80 USD/kg (ref.14) and 0.11 USD/kg (ref.15). Then the levelized cost 
of electrolyte throughout the plant’s lifespan are calculated as follows:

3. ECR//HOR coupled with HER//AEM//MOR configuration 
The main reaction to produce HCOO− in HER//AEM//MOR is given as follows: 
 Overall: CH3OH + OH− → HCOO−+ 2H2 
 Cathode: 4H2O + 4e− → 2H2 + 4OH−  
 Anode: CH3OH + 5OH− − 4e− → HCOO− + 4H2O 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We considered a side reaction observed 
in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1: 
 Overall: CH3OH + H2O → CO2 + 3H2 
 Cathode: 6H2O + 6e− → 3H2 + 6OH− 
 Anode: CH3OH + 6OH− − 6e− → CO2 + 5H2O 
The process flow diagram for ECR//HOR coupled with HER//AEM//MOR configuration developed 
in Aspen Plus is shown on Supplementary Fig. 49. For the HER//AEM//MOR configuration, 0.1 M 
KOH is used as electrolyte on both anode and cathode side. The generated H2 at cathode side is fed 
to the anode side of ECR//HOR, eliminating the need of buying fresh H2 from market. In addition, 
the pure CO2 generated from the anode side of HER//AEM//MOR will be sent to the cathode side 
of ECR//HOR to reduce CO 2 consumption. The HCOO−  generated at the anode side of 
HER//AEM//MOR is 0.2  M and  will undergo electrodialysis to obtain HCOOH, as well as 
increasing its concentration to 2 M for efficient liquid separation. A ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1 = 90%: 10% 
was used when establishing material balance for HER//AEM//MOR while a list of ratios including 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 100%: 0%: 0%, 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 90%: 8%: 2%, 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 80%: 18%: 2%, 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 70%: 28%: 2% were used when establishing material balance for ECR//HOR to 
investigate the influence of faradiac efficiency on the TEA performance of the couple d 
configuration. As the total HCOOH amount obtained from the coupled configuration is fixed at 90 
kmol/hr, the HCOOH obtained from the ECR//HOR and HER//AEM//MOR under the four 
scenarios are 62069 + 27931 mol/hr, 60000 + 30000 mol/hr, 57600 + 32400 mol/hr, and 54782.6 + 
35217.4 mol/hr, respectively. In the following calculations, we use the second scenario as example 
to demonstrate the calculation procedures.

For HER//AEM//MOR configuration, we estimated the membrane cost based on a unit price of 400 
USD/m2 (Ref.8), a lifetime of 5 years 8, and a discount rate of 7%. The resulting CRF is 0.2439. A 
current density of 0.1 A/cm2 observed from experiments is adopted for calculation.

(8) Electrolyte Cost 
For ECR//HOR configuration, electrolyte including K 2SO4 and H 2SO4 is neither consumed or 
generated during the entire production cycle. For HER//AEM//MOR configuration, the OH − 
generated at the cathode side via the main reaction is transported to the anode side. The flowrates 
of electrolyte required to sustain the desired concentrat ions (i.e., 1M K + and 0.01M H + for 
ECR//HOR and 1M OH− for HER//AEM//MOR) are obtained from aspen simulations, and they are 
shown in Supplementary Table 9. 
We assume a 48 hr stock of electrolyte to account for production fluctuations, and the prices for 
K2SO4, H2SO4, and KOH set to 0.80  USD/kg (ref.14) and 0.11 USD/kg (ref.15), and 0.63 USD/kg

2. Finally, I noticed that on page 12 of the manuscript, it mentions that the formic acid production 
concentration is 0.1 M. Thus, the reviewer suggests that the authors should base their TEA 
calculations for formic acid separation and concentration on this experimentally obtained value to 
ensure accurate computations. 
 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this valuable suggestion. In the revised techno -economic analysis, the 
downstream separation section was recalculated based on this dilute  HCOOH feed concentration 
(0.1 M) . A separation process involving electrodialysis followed b y extraction–distillation was 
employed. The liquid mixture containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis 
to reduce the water amount for efficient extractive distillation. Then , the 2 M HCOOH first 
processed through an extraction distill ation column (EDC), followed by entrainer recover column 
(ERC), as shown on Supplementary Fig. 46.  Please check the revised Supplementary Note 2 for 
details, also copied below.  
 
Supplementary Information, Supplementary Note 2 
(4) Electrodialysis process for concentrating formic acid 
To improve the separation efficiency and reduce energy consumption, the liquid mixture coming 
out from the electrolyzer is first concentrated via electrodialysis to increase the HCOOH 
concentration from 0.1 M to 2 M. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin 9, the CAPEX and 
OPEX associated with the electrodialysis are estimated at 0.06 USD kg −1 and 0.13 USD kg −1, 
respectively. For a fixed production goal of 90 kmol/hr HCOOH, aspen simulations indicated a mass 
flowrate of 4142.31 kg/hr. 
𝐿𝐶𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑑𝑖𝑎𝑙𝑦𝑠𝑖𝑠

3.31 × 104 𝑡𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒 = 129.99 ( 𝑈𝑆𝐷

(6) Liquid Production Separation 
For the liquid product separation, an extraction -distillation approach was adopted based on a 
previously reported process design12. In this approach, the 2 M HCOOH first processed through an 
extraction distillation column (EDC), followed by entrainer recover column (ERC), as shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 46. Sulfolane was selected as the entrainer for H2O/HCOOH maximum-boiling 
azeotropic system. The sulfolane price is set to 2.2 USD/kg (ref.13). Rigorous extraction-distillation 
simualtions were performed in Aspen Plus, and it is shown that formic acid can be concentrated to 
over 98 wt% via this two -step separation, meeting commercial purity standards. The process 
parameters of the two columns, including reflux ratio, feed location, number of plates, operating 
pressure, were consistent with the literature 6. We assume a lifespan of 20 years for the extraction-
distillation unit and a discount rate of 7%.

Reviewer #4 
 
1. In the updated TEA calculations, I believe there is a significant error in the calculation of the 
Capex for the CO2 electrolyzer. Specifically, on page 50 of the response document where the authors 
shown their calculation of the area -normalized Capex based on the reported Capex of traditional 
PEM water electrolyzers (~ $560 USD/kW), they assume a current density of 0.17 A/cm2. This is 
incorrect. Conventional PEM electrolyzers should have a reference current density of closer to 1.7 
A/cm2, not 0.17 A/cm2. As a result, the area-based Capex should be $16,184 USD/m^2, NOT $1,618 
USD/m^2. As an aside, the authors should be careful with their dimensional analysis and more 
clearly spell out the conversion from kW to W and cm^2 to m^2. 
 
Response 
 
Thanks for your suggestion. The 0.17 A/cm2 and $1,618 USD/m2 was from the reference (Energy 
Fuels 2023, 37, 23, 17997 –18008, Supplementary Information Page 12 , Fig. R3 ), however, the 
reference date maybe not suitable for our system. We have revised the tec hno-economic analysis 
using Aspen -based process simulations, and the Capex calculation has also been updated 
accordingly. Please check Supplementary Note 2 for details, also copied below. 
 
Fig. R3. | The parameters used for Capex was according to the reference (Energy Fuels 2023, 37, 
23, 17997–18008, Supplementary Information Page 12).

 Supplementary Note. 2 | Techno-Economic Assessment 
We conducted techno -economic assessment (TEA) for various electrolyzer systems presented in 
this work under a product ion goal of 90 kmol HCOOH/hr and an annual operating time of 8000 
hours (i.e., 90 tonnes/day)  (Supplementary Table 6). The lifespan of the plant is set to 20 years 6. 
Capital expenditure (CAPEX) and operating expense (OPEX) are both presented in the form of 
levelized production cost of HCOOH (denoted as LC, with a unit of USD/tonne HCOOH). Aspen 
Plus was utilized to establish material balance and provide necessary data to support calculations in 
TEA. Gas mixture containing unreacted CO 2 is recovered using pressure swing adsorpting (PSA) 
while extractive distillation is employed to obtain 95  wt% HCOOH product. The water recovered 
from extractive distillation was reused to sustain electrolyte concentration, reducing the cost of 
water. 
1. ECR//HOR and ECR//CEM//HOR configurations 
In the system based on HOR, the main reaction to produce HCOOH in thes e two configurations is 
given as follows: 
 Overall: CO2 + H2 → HCOOH 
 Cathode: CO2 + 2H+ + 2e− → HCOOH 
 Anode:H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We also considered two side reactions 
observed in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1 
and 𝑄𝑠2, respectively: 
 Overall: H2 →  H2 
 Cathode: 2H+ + 2e− → H2 
 Anode: H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
 
 Overall: CO2 + H2 → CO + H2O 
 Cathode: CO2 + 2H+ + 2e− → CO + H2O 
 Anode: H2 − 2e− → 2H+ 
The process flow diagram for ECR//HOR configuration developed in Aspen Plus is shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 44. Specifically, electrolyte containing 0.5 M K2SO4 and 0.005 M H2SO4 is fed 
to the electrolyzer while H 2 and CO2 are fed to the anode side and cathode side, respectively. The 
unreacted H2 coming out from the anode side is recycled and the gas mixture coming out from the 
cathode side goes through pressure swing adsorption to recovery unreacted CO2. The liquid mixture 
containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis to reduce the water amount for 
efficient extractive distillation. For ECR//HOR, a ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 90%: 8%: 2% was used 
when establishing material balance. 
Similarly, the process flow diagram for ECR//CEM//HOR configuration developed in Aspen Plus 
is shown on Supplementary Fig. 45.  For ECR//CEM//HOR, a ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 =
52%: 46%: 2% was used when establishing material balance.

(4) Electrodialysis process for concentrating formic acid 
To improve the separation efficiency and reduce energy consumption, the liquid mixture coming 
out from the electrolyzer is first concentrated via electrodialysis to increase the HCOOH 
concentration from 0.1 M to 2 M. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin 9, the CAPEX and 
OPEX associated with the electrodialysis are estimated at 0.06 USD kg −1 and 0.13 USD kg −1, 
respectively. For a fixed production goal of 90 kmol/hr HCOOH, aspen simulations indicated a mass 
flowrate of 4142.31 kg/hr. 
𝐿𝐶𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑑𝑖𝑎𝑙𝑦𝑠𝑖𝑠

3.31 × 104 𝑡𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒 = 59.99 ( 𝑈𝑆𝐷
𝑡𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒)

𝑂𝑃𝐸𝑋 =
0.13 𝑈𝑆𝐷
𝑘𝑔 × 4142.31 𝑘𝑔
ℎ𝑟 × 8000ℎ𝑟
3.31 × 104 𝑡𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒 = 129.99 ( 𝑈𝑆𝐷
𝑡𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒) 
(5) Cathode Gas Separation 
Pressure swing adsorption (PSA) is a mature industrial technology that offers an efficient, cost -
effective, and space-saving approach for gas separation10. In CO2 electrolysis processes, the reactor 
exhaust typically contains a mixture of unreacted CO2, target gas products, and by-product hydrogen. 
A comparable industrial scenario is biogas upgrading, where raw biogas composed mainly of 
methane and CO 2 is purified u sing PSA systems. Insights from commercial biogas upgrading 
projects have been used to estimate the capital and operational requirements for PSA units in CO 2 
electrolysis applications. In line with these industrial benchmarks, we adopt a reference capital cost 
of 1,989,043 USD per 1,000 m 3/h of throughput, with a scaling factor of 0.7 and a capacity factor 
of 0.9 to account for economies of scale and operation flexibility11. The primary operating expense 
for PSA is electricity consumption, estimated at 0.25 kWh per cubic meter of gas treated 11. 
Additional costs related to gas compression and storage downstream of the separation unit are 
assumed negligible in this analysis. The volume of gas mixture coming out from cathode is obtained 
via Aspen Plus simulations. The volume of gas mixture under ECR//HOR configuration is 1000.12 
m3/h while the volume of gas mixture under ECR//CEM//HOR configuration is 2803.87 m 3/h. We 
assume a lifespan of  20 years for the PSA unit and a discount rate of 7%.

(6) Liquid Production Separation 
For the liquid product separat ion, an extraction -distillation approach was adopted based on a 
previously reported process design12. In this approach, the 2 M HCOOH first processed through an 
extraction distillation column (EDC), followed by entrainer recover column (ERC), as shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 46. Sulfolane was selected as the entrainer for H2O/HCOOH maximum-boiling 
azeotropic system. The sulfolane price is set to 2.2 USD/kg (ref.13). Rigorous extraction-distillation 
simualtions were performed in Aspen Plus, and it is shown that formic acid can be concentrated to 
over 98 wt% via this two -step separation, meeting commercial purity standards. The process 
parameters of the two columns, including reflux ratio, feed location, number of plates, operating 
pressure, were consistent with the literature 6. We assume a lifespan of 20 years for the extraction-
distillation unit and a discount rate of 7%. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 46 |  Process flow diagram of the extraction -distillation separation of 
formic acid. The 2 M HCOOH solution is first processed through an extraction distillation column 
(EDC) and subsequently through an entrainer recovery column (ERC) for solvent regeneration and 
HCOOH purification.

2. ECR//OER and ECR//CEM//OER configurations 
In the system based on OER, the main reaction to produce HCOOH in these two configurations is 
given as follows: 
 Overall: 2CO2 + 2H2O(l) → 2HCOOH + O2  
 Cathode: 2CO2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2HCOOH

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 134 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We also considered two side reactions 
observed in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1 
and 𝑄𝑠2, respectively: 
 Overall: 2H2O →  2H2 + O2   
 Cathode: 4H+ + 4e− → 2H2 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
 
 Overall: 2CO2 → 2CO + O2 
 Cathode: 2CO2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2CO + 2H2O 
 Anode: 2H2O − 4e− → O2 + 4H+ 
The process flow diagram for ECR//OER configuration developed in Aspen Plus is shown on 
Supplementary Fig. 47. Specifically, electrolyte containing 0.5 M K2SO4 and 0.005 M H2SO4 is fed 
to the electrolyzer while CO2 is fed to the cathode side. The gas mixture coming out from the cathode 
side goes through pressure swing adsorption to recovery unreacted CO 2. The liquid mixture 
containing 0.1 M HCOOH is concentrated to 2 M via electrodialysis to reduce the water amount for 
efficient extractive distillation. To account for the oxidation of HCOOH at  anode, a ratio of 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 54%: 8%: 2% was used when establishing material balance for ECR//OER. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 47 | Process flow diagram of ECR//OER.

3. ECR//HOR coupled with HER//AEM//MOR configuration 
The main reaction to produce HCOO− in HER//AEM//MOR is given as follows: 
 Overall: CH3OH + OH− → HCOO−+ 2H2 
 Cathode: 4H2O + 4e− → 2H2 + 4OH−  
 Anode: CH3OH + 5OH− − 4e− → HCOO− + 4H2O 
where the number of electrons transferred is denoted as 𝑄𝑚. We considered a side reaction observed 
in experiments, and the corresponding number of electrons transferred are denoted as 𝑄𝑠1: 
 Overall: CH3OH + H2O → CO2 + 3H2 
 Cathode: 6H2O + 6e− → 3H2 + 6OH− 
 Anode: CH3OH + 6OH− − 6e− → CO2 + 5H2O 
The process flow diagram for ECR//HOR coupled with HER//AEM//MOR configuration developed 
in Aspen Plus is shown on Supplementary Fig. 49. For the HER//AEM//MOR configuration, 0.1 M 
KOH is used as electrolyte on both anode and cathode side. The generated H2 at cathode side is fed

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 139 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
to the anode side of ECR//HOR, eliminating the need of buying fresh H 2 from market. In addition, 
the pure CO2 generated from the anode side of HER//AEM//MOR will be sent to the cathode side 
of ECR//HOR to reduce CO 2 consumption. The HCOO−  generated at the anode side of  
HER//AEM//MOR is 0.2  M and will undergo electrodialysis to obtain HCOOH, as well as 
increasing its concentration to 2 M for efficient liquid separation. A ratio of 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1 = 90%: 10% 
was used when establishing material balance for HER//AEM//MOR while a list of ratios including 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 100%: 0%: 0%, 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 90%: 8%: 2%, 𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 80%: 18%: 2%, 
𝑄𝑚: 𝑄𝑠1: 𝑄𝑠2 = 70%: 28%: 2% were used when establishing material balance for ECR//H OR to 
investigate the influence of faradiac efficiency on the TEA performance of the coupled 
configuration. As the total HCOOH amount obtained from the coupled configuration is fixed at 90 
kmol/hr, the HCOOH obtained from the ECR//HOR and HER//AEM//MOR und er the four 
scenarios are 62069 + 27931 mol/hr, 60000 + 30000 mol/hr, 57600 + 32400 mol/hr, and 54782.6 + 
35217.4 mol/hr, respectively. In the following calculations, we use the second scenario as example 
to demonstrate the calculation procedures.

For ECR //HOR configuration, electrolyte including K 2SO4 and H 2SO4 is neither consumed or 
generated during the entire production cycle. For HER//AEM//MOR configuration, the OH − 
generated at the cathode side via the main reaction is transported to the anode side. T he flowrates 
of electrolyte required to sustain the desired concentrations (i.e., 1M K + and 0.01M H + for 
ECR//HOR and 1M OH− for HER//AEM//MOR) are obtained from aspen simulations, and they are 
shown in Supplementary Table 9.

(ref.17), respectively. Then the levelized cost of electrolyte throughout th e plant ’s lifespan are 
calculated as follows:

2. Supplementary Fig. 39 (sensitivity analysis)- I find it confusing to interpret this figure. I suggest 
that the authors re -draw it in the form of a more traditional “tornado plot” where the baseline, 
“high”, and “low” values of the dependent variable are all plotted on the same axis instead of two 
identical axes as was done here. Furthermore, I suggest the autho rs plot the levelized cost of F A 
production as the dependent variable being plotted rather than NPV . NPV is not very useful on its 
own since it is so dependent on plant size. 
 
Response 
We appreciate the reviewer’s constructive suggestion. In the revised ma nuscript, we have redrawn 
the sensitivity analysis as a traditional tornado plot (new Supplementary Fig. 50), where the baseline, 
low, and high values of the dependent variable are plotted on a single shared horizontal axis for 
clearer comparison. This revised presentation highlights that electricity price is the dominant factors 
influencing production cost.

3. Regarding scalability, it is encouraging that the new larger cell also demonstrated high FE 
towards F A, but the authors should also add overlaid polarization (current density vs. voltage) 
curves for both the 0.5 cm × 2 cm and 2 cm × 2 cm cells. This will be very helpful to see how the  
performance is affected by the increased electrode size. 
 
Response 
We thank the reviewer for this helpful suggestion. Following the comment, we compared the 
polarization curves of the 0.5 cm × 2 cm and 2 cm × 2 cm cells (Supplementary Fig. 30b). The 0.5 
cm × 2 cm cell exhibits a lower cell voltage compared to the 2 cm × 2 cm cell at the same current 
densities. This observation indicates that increasing the electrode size slightly increases the ohmic 
and mass transport resistance. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 30b | Polarization curve comparison of ECR//HOR electrolyzers with 0.5 cm 
× 2 cm and 2 cm × 2 cm electrode area, respectively. No iR compensation was applied. 
 
4. Regarding CO poisoning of the Pt anode catalyst: I do not agree with the authors' 
interpretation of their new results. Supplementary Fig. 28 clearly shows a CO stripping peak around 
+0.8 V vs. RHE; below this potential it is likely that Pt has a difficult time doing HOR (as is well 
known in H2 fuel cell literature). That means that the anode probably needs at least +0.8 V of extra 
overpotential to oxidize CO and be active towards HOR. i.e., at high enough voltage, the poison 
will be removed, but device efficiency suffers from the higher voltage. 
 
Response

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 148 -->

P
A
G
E
 
 
 
 
Thanks for your suggestions. We agree with the reviewer that if Pt were poisoned by CO, HOR 
would only occur at much higher overpotentials (e.g., >0.8 V vs. RHE). However, in Supplementary 
Fig. 33, our intention was not to show that our Pt/C catalysts are resistance to CO poisoning, but 
rather to demonstrate that CO generated at the cathode does not cross over to the anode and poison 
Pt/C. To verify this, we tested the CV of the Pt/C anode after the stability test (blue curve) and did 
not observe a CO oxida tion peak, indicating that no significant CO adsorption occurred on the 
catalyst surface after the test. 
 
We then performed a control experiment demonstrating that if the Pt/C catalyst adsorbs CO, a 
distinct CO oxidation peak emerges at approximately 0.88 V vs. RHE. The red curve corresponds 
to the CV of the Pt/C electrode after CO adsorption. In the CO stripping experiment, the CO 
oxidation peak disappears in the second scan due to the removal of CO  (grey dot line) , thereby 
confirming the position of the CO oxidation peak. We have modified the statement in the main text 
and figure caption of Supplementary Fig. 33 to make it more clearly. 
 
Main text, Page 14 
No CO oxidation peak was observed on the electrode after stability test, indicating that no 
significant CO adsorption occurred on the Pt/C anode during long -term operation 
(Supplementary Fig. 33) . These results demonstrate that the CO generated at the cathode did 
not crossover to the anode to cause CO poisoning under the tested flow cell conditions. 
 
 
 
Supplementary Fig. 33 | CO stripping voltammetry of the Pt/C catalysts. Cyclic voltammetry 
(CV) was first performed by scanning from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at 20 mV s−1 to detect any CO 
oxidation peak of the Pt/C electrode after stability tests  (blue curve). No CO oxidation peak was 
observed, indicating that no significant CO adsorption occurred on the Pt/C anode during long-term 
operation. 
Subsequently, high-purity CO gas was bubbled through the electrolyte for 20 min while holding the 
working electrode potential at 0.05 V vs. RHE to ensure sufficient CO adsorption. The cell was then 
flushed with fresh N 2-purged electrolyte for 10 min to remove residual CO. Finally, CO stripping 
was carried out by CV scanning twice from 0.05 V to 1.55 V vs. RHE at a scan rate of 20 mV s−1. A 
clear CO oxidation peak appeared at approximately 0.88 V during the first CO stripping scan (red 
curve) and disappeared in the second scan (grey dot line). Abovementioned test confirmed that the

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 149 -->

Pt surface remained fully activ e for CO oxidation and was not poisoned by CO generated at the 
cathode during long-term operation21-23.
