REVIEWER COMMENTS 
Reviewer #1 
 
The questions have been properly addressed. I believe this work is ready for publication now. 
 
Response: 
Thanks for your support. 
 
 
Reviewer #3 
 
I have carefully reviewed the authors' detailed content regarding the TEA results and appreciate 
their contributions in this area, which have enhanced the rigor of the scientific research. However, 
I believe the authors have not fully addressed the issues I raised. My primary concern lies in the cost 
consumption during the concentration process of the dilute formic acid stream obtained in this 
experiment. In their response to Comment 2, the authors cited content from Reference 9 and 
Reference 12 to support their analysis of the concentration cost of the formic acid product. However, 
the reviewer noted that in Reference 9, the original authors used a formic acid feed concentration of 
10%wt, rather than the concentration of 0.1 mol/L ≈ 0.46%wt obtained in the authors' article. 
Therefore, the reviewer considers it inappropriate to directly apply the energy consumption data 
from this concentration process. The current data can not support the results presented in the article. 
 
Response 
We sincerely appreciate the reviewer’s support. Following your suggestion, we have revised the cost 
associated with the concentration process based on both the reference
1 methods and our 
experimental data. Regarding the CAPEX of the electrodialysis process, since the cost is highly  
related to the membrane area, we adjusted the membrane area according to our HCOOH 
concentration, as well as the electrodialysis current density and current efficiency reported in the 
reference
2 to concentrate 0.1 M HCOOH . For the electrodialysis stack voltage, which depends 
strongly on the ionic conductivity of the electrolyte, we used a 0.5 M K2SO4-based electrolyte feed. 
As the input and output electrolyte conductivities in our system were similar to those in the 
reference
1, we corrected only the OPEX by adjusting the energy consumption according to the 
current efficiency. Please check the Supplementary Note 2 for details, also copied below. 
 
Supplementary Note 2 
(4) Electrodialysis process for concentrating formic acid 
To improve the separation efficiency and reduce energy consumption, the liquid mixture coming 
out from the electrolyzer is first concentrated via electrodialysis to increase the HCOOH 
concentration from 0.1 M to 2  M. According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin 1, the CAPEX of 
electrodialysis is closely related to the membrane required, which can be calculated as follows:

𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖

volume flow (L/s) in the stack, 𝑖𝑖 is the current density (A/m 2), 𝑖𝑖 is the current efficiency, and 𝐶𝐶𝑓𝑓 
and 𝐶𝐶𝑑𝑑 are the concentrations of HCOOH in the feed and dilute stream (mol equiv/L), respectively. 
The dilute stream is assumed to free of formic acid.  To account for low feed concentration of

It is important to note that the energy consumption in electrodialysis processes is strongly dependent 
on the current density, current efficiency, and electrolyte conductivity. The current density and 
electrolyte conductivity directly influence the cell voltage, while the current efficiency determines 
the overall energy consumption—higher current efficiency results in lower energy consumption to 
produce concentrated products of the same quality. The input and output electrolyte conductivities 
of our system (0.5 M K
2SO4: ~80 mS/cm; 10 wt% HCOOH: ~7.8 mS/cm) were comparable to those 
of the reference (10% KCOOH: ~50 mS/cm; 30% HCOOH: ~10.5 mS/cm).3,4 Moreover, since our 
system operated at a lower current density, the overall cell voltage should be comparable to or lower 
than that of the reference system. Consequently, the electricity consumption related to the OPEX of 
electrodialysis was only adjusted based on the current efficiency:

0.6
× 2.5 𝑘𝑘𝑘𝑘ℎ
𝑘𝑘𝑘𝑘 = 3.13 �𝑘𝑘𝑘𝑘ℎ

Reviewer #4 
 
1. Regarding the Capex of the CO2 electrolyzer, I still maintain that the authors are making a major 
mistake that results in significantly underestimating the value of the electrolyzer Capex. I see that 
they are using the same input numbers from a previous paper (Energy & Fuels, 2023, 37, 23, 17997–
18008, Supplementary Information Page 12, Fig. R3), but when I looked deeper into this, it seems 
that the assumption of a reference current density of 0.17 A/cm2 in the Energy & Fuels paper is 
incorrect. It’s also worth pointing out that their Eqn. (6) is missing a term for the total cell area, 
another sloppy mistake. From page 4 of the cited 2019 NREL Report (“Manufacturing Cost Analysis 
for Proton Exchange Membrane Water Electrolyzers”, an excellent resource), one can clearly see 
the reference voltage of 1.7 V , and the current density should be 1.7 A/cm2, not 0.17 A/c m2. A 
current density of 1.7 A/cm2 is much more typical of a PEM water electrolyzer, which is the basis 
of the NREL study. From all previous electrolyzer TEA studies by others or that I have done myself, 
the electrolyzer Capex is based on normalization of the total electrolyzer system Capex by the rated 
power (i.e. Area x reference operating current density x reference voltage), not an arbitrarily low  
current density like 0.17 A/cm2. Why would a reference current density of 0.17 A/cm2 be chosen 
when the PEM electrolyzer typically operates 1.5-2.0 A/cm2? Thus, it seems evident that there is a 
mistake in the Energy & Fuels paper (and hence, the current m anuscript) that leads to the CO2 
electrolyzer Capex (expressed in $/m^2) being 1 order of magnitude lower than it should be. In the 
pdf version of my comments, I've included the reference table from the 2019 NREL report cited in 
the Energy & Fuels paper that clearly shows a reference current density of 1.7 A/cm2, not 0.17 
A/cm2. 
 
Response 
We sincerely appreciate the reviewer’s suggestion. We have revised the CAPEX of the electrolyzer 
according to your advice, which has made our analysis more rigorous. Specifically, we first 
converted the power -normalized capital cost (USD/kW) reported in the NREL  into an area-
normalized cost (USD/m
2) based on the reported cell voltage (1.7 V) and current density (1.7 A/cm2). 
Then, using our total current and operating current density, we calculated the required electrolyzer 
area to estimate the total capital cost. Please refer to Supplementary Note 2 for detailed calculations 
(also provided below). In addition, we have revised the techno- economic analysis discussion to 
emphasize the significance of the electrolyzer CAPEX. The corresponding revisions can be found 
in the main text (Page 16), also copied below.

Main text, Page 16 
A comprehensive TEA was carried out  (Supplementary Note 2, Supplementary Figs. 44- 50). 
Assuming a CO2 feedstock cost of 40 USD ton −1 and a methanol feedstock cost of 470 USD 
ton−1, and an electricity price of 0.03 USD kWh −1, integration with a separation process 
involving electrodialysis followed by extraction–distillation yields an estimated levelized 
production cost of formic acid of approximately 1182.5 USD ton−1 (Fig. 5d). The major cost 
components contributing to this value include separation cost, electrolyzer capital cost , and 
electricity expense. Note that because electrolysis was conducted at a low current density (100 
mA cm−2), achieving the targeted production rate requires a larger electrolyzer area (electrolyzer

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 157 -->

5 
 
cost5: 310 USD kW−1 at 1.7 V and 1.7A cm −2, corresponding to 8959 USD m−2), leading to a 
higher capital investment. Although the production cost slightly higher than the current market 
price6 (830 USD ton−1), it is expected to decrease further with reductions in electrolyzer capital 
cost and electricity price (Supplementary Fig. 50). In addition, compared with direct H2 supply 
(Supplementary Note 4), this approach reduces the costs associated with H 2 transport and 
storage (factors not considered in our TEA) and also enhances operational safety. Future work 
should therefore focus on increasing the electrolysis current density to reduce the electrolyzer 
capital cost and enhance the overall process economics. 
 
 
Fig. 5 | d, TEA analysis of the integrated system for HCOOH production. 
 
 
2. Regarding CO poisoning: my original point in my previous comment was that the CO2 
electrolyzer is operating at such high voltages (4- 5 V) that it is not surprising that there is no CO 
oxidation signal seen during CO stripping voltammetry measurement recorded at the end of the 
durability test. Under these conditions, the anode is likely polarized to such high values that any CO 
that crosses over from the cathode is easily oxidized and therefore doesn’t show up in the subsequent 
CO stripping voltammetry experiments. It may be true that CO poisoning isn’t occurring at these 
conditions, but that’s only because a very high cell voltage (and thus lower conversion efficiency) 
was used. The lack of a CO stripping peak after the stability test doesn’t prove that CO is not crossing 
over, and it doesn’t mean that CO poisoning is not an issue. I would guess that the use of a more 
CO tolerant anode catalyst like PtRu would lead to significantly reduced cell voltage. Thus, I still 
disagree with the authors’ statement that there is no CO crossover in their cell in this line: “ These 
results demonstrate that the CO generated at the cathode did not crossover to the anode to cause CO 
poisoning under the tested flow cell conditions.” At the least, I feel like this statement is v ery 
misleading and should be changed. 
 
Response 
We respectfully disagree with the reviewer’s comment that the CO 2 electrolyzer operates at an 
excessively high voltage (4–5 V). In fact, during the durability test, our electrolyzer with a 2 mm 
electrolyte flow layer exhibited a cell voltage of only approximately 1.7 V at 100 mA cm−2 (Fig. 4d 
and f). We acknowledge the reviewer ’s concern that the original statement, “ These results 
demonstrate that the CO generated at the cathode did not crossover to the anode to cause CO 
poisoning under the tested flow cell conditions,” may cause misleading, and we agree that it is not

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 158 -->

6 
 
appropriate to conclude that no CO crossover occurred from the cathode to the anode. Accordingly, 
we have revised it to: “Together with the stable low cell voltage (~1.7 V at 100 mA cm −2, Fig. 4d 
and f), these results indicate that the CO generated at the cathode has a negligible impact on the 
overall cell voltage and electrochemical performance.” (also copied below). 
 
 
Fig. 4 | d, Stability test of the thin electrolyte flow layer ECR//HOR electrolyzer at a current density 
of 100 mA cm−2. f, Stability test of the thin electrolyte flow layer ECR//HOR electrolyzer at a current 
density of 100 mA cm−2 using acidic humidification strategy. 
 
Main text, Page 14 
Together with the stable low cell voltage ( ~1.7 V at 100 mA cm
−2, Fig. 4d and f ), these results 
indicate that the CO generated at the cathode has a negligible impact on the overall cell voltage and 
electrochemical performance.

as Electrode Materials. J. Electrochem. Soc. 171 (2024).

REVIEWER COMMENTS  
 
Reviewer #3 
The economic assessment of formic acid concentration in the current TEA is unreasonable and 
lacks detailed, solid information to support the accuracy of the adopted methodology.  
The systems documented in the literature typically involve the separation of HCOONa solutions. 
In contrast, this study deals with a mixture of K2SO4 and HCOOH. These are entirely different 
reaction systems. Consequently, the energy consumption values reported in the literature cannot be 
directly applied to this specific mixture. 
It is currently unclear to the reader how the electrodialysis method is implemented to concentrate 
the K2SO4/HCOOH mixture, particularly given the complicat ion of anion crossover. Therefore, 
the reviewer finds the current calculation method to be unfounded. 
The approach lacks support from both relevant literature and any provided experimental  or 
simulation data. As a result, it is my opinion that the current methodology cannot reliably support 
the presented overall economic calculations. 
 
Response 
We sincerely appreciate the reviewer’s constructive suggestions. In response, we have added a 
detailed separation process based on electrodialysis for concentrating formic acid. We agree with 
the reviewer’s opinion that our system differs from typical literature examples involving 
HCOONa separation, as our system contains K2SO4 and HCOOH. Accordingly, we have modified 
the separation process. First, we neutralize the solution with KOH. Then, we utilize a modified 
anion-exchange membrane designed to block sulfate transport while selectively allowing HCOO − 
passage due to their diff ering charges. The modification cost was estimated at 50% of the base 
membrane cost. Similar membranes and processes have been employed in wastewater treatment, 
supporting the feasibility of our proposed design  (References: Membranes 12, 610 (2022); AIChE 
J. 57, 2070–2078 (2010); Chem. Eng. J. 333, 162–169 (2018)). 
Regarding energy consumption, we have corrected our calculation based on electrolyte 
conductivity and current efficiency. Both the inlet and outlet electrolyte conductivity and the 
current efficiency were obtained from the literature. While this may not represent the exact real -
world energy consumption, the estimate is reasonable  because the cell voltage for electrodialysis 
is highly related with  the electrolyte conductivity and current efficiency. The energy consumption 
in a practical electrodialysis separation procedure is directly proportional to the total current 
flowing through the stack and the voltage drop between the electrodes  (Reference: Strathmann, H. 
(1986). Electrodialysis. In: Bungay, P.M., Lonsdale, H.K., de Pinho, M.N. (eds) Synthetic 
Membranes: Science, Engineering and Applications . NATO ASI Series, vol 181. Springer, 
Dordrecht.). It can be expressed as: 
𝐸 =  𝑛𝐼2𝑅𝑡

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 161 -->

2 
 
where E is the energy consumption,  n is the number o f cells in the stack,   I is the electric current 
through the stack, R is the cell resistance, and t is time. 
The electric current is proportional to the number of ions transferred through the ion -exchange 
membranes from the feed stream to the concentrated solution: 
𝐼 =  𝑧𝐹𝑄∆𝑐
𝜉  
where z is the electrochemical valence, F is the Faraday constant, Q is the total feed solution flow 
rate, Δc is the concentration difference between the inlet and outlet  streams, and ξ is the current 
efficiency. Thus, the energy consumption is a function of the current, the electrical resistance of 
the stack (including the membrane and electrolyte solution resistance), current efficiency, and the 
concentration difference between the inlet and outlet streams.  Consequently, our appr oximation 
based on the solution conductivity and current efficiency is reasonable. In addition, to illustrate the 
electrodialysis process, we have provided two schematics showing electrodialysis for 
concentrating K 2SO4/HCOOH and KOH/HCOOH mixtures and have  performed recalculations 
accordingly (Supplementary Figs. 46 and 51) . We have also incorporated additional discussion in 
the main text to emphasize the significance of the separation step. Please refer to the main text and 
Supplementary Note 2 for full details, which are also provided below for your convenience.  
Main text, Page 15 
A comprehensive TEA was carried out  (Supplementary Note  2, Supplementary Figs. 44 -52). 
Assuming a CO2 feedstock cost of 40 USD ton−1 and a methanol feedstock cost of 470 USD ton−1, 
and an electricity price of 0.03 USD kWh −1, integration with a separation process involving 
electrodialysis followed by extraction–distillation yields an estimated levelized production cost of 
formic acid of approximately 2041.1 USD ton −1 (Fig. 5d).  The major cost components 
contributing to this value include: separation cost, electrolyzer capital cost, and electricity expense. 
The separation cost ( 1267.7 USD ton −1) accounted for approximately half of the total cost. This 
resulted from the low initial p roduct concentration (0.1 M)  and the presence of a KOH and 
formate mixture from the HER//AEM//MOR electrolyzer , which required an energy -intensive 
electrodialysis process to concentrate the produced formate to 2 M. 
 
Main text, Page 16 
Future work should therefore focus on increasing the electrolysis current density to lower 
electrolyzer capital costs and on developing new separation methods to reduce separation costs. 
 
Supplementary Information, Page 52 
(4) Electrodialysis process for concentrating formic acid 
To improve separation efficiency and reduce energy consumption, the liquid mixture from the 
electrolyzer is concentrated via electrodialysis to increase the HCOOH concentration from 0.1 M 
to 2 M, as shown in Supplementary Fig. 46. Specifically, the HCOOH-containing liquid mixture is

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 162 -->

3 
 
first neutralized with KOH to obtain a solution of potassiu m formate and potassium sulfate. A 
three-compartment electrodialysis with bipolar membranes (EDBPM) is then employed to 
reconvert potassium formate into formic acid, following the process demonstrated by Mahinder 
Ramdin12. To mitigate anion crossover, a mod ified anion -exchange membrane with a high 
formate/sulfate selectivity ratio is required 13-15. In this work, the TEA model of the EDBPM 
process developed by Mahinder Ramdin12 is revised by assuming a 1.5 -fold higher AEM cost to 
account for this membrane modification. The KOH generated in the base compartment is recycled, 
whereas the formic acid produ ced in the acid compartment is directed to extractive distillation to 
achieve 95 wt%. To maintain electrolyte circulation in the dilute compartment, excess  K2SO4 
formed from the reaction between KOH and H2SO4 is removed by reducing the electrolyte volume 
by approximately 1%, given that the initial electrolyte composition is 0.5M K 2SO4 and 0.005M 
H2SO4 as electrolyte. Fresh water and H2SO4 purchased from the market are then added to restore 
the target electroly te concentrations. It should be noted that the KOH used to neutralize H 2SO4 
cannot be regenerated during electrodialysis due to charge -balance constraints; therefore, 
additional KOH must be supplied from the market to sustain continuous operation. 
 
Supplementary Fig. 46 | Process for concentrating formic acid using a three -compartment 
EDBPM. 
 
According to the study by Mahinder Ramdin 12, the CAPEX of electrodialysis is closely related to 
the membrane required, which can be calculated as follows: 
𝐴 = 𝑛𝐹𝑄𝑠𝑡(𝐶𝑓 − 𝐶𝑑)

and 𝐶𝑑 are the concentrations of HCOOH in the feed and dilute stream (mol equiv/L), respectively. 
The dilute stream is assumed to free of for mic acid.  To account for low feed concentration of 
HCOOH, an current density of 150 A/m2 and an current efficiency of 60% are adopted from Ref.16

It is important to note that the energy consumption in electrodialysis processes is strongly 
dependent on the current density, current efficiency, and electrolyte conductivity. The c urrent 
density and electrolyte conductivity directly influence the cell voltage, while the current efficiency 
determines the overall energy consumption —higher current efficiency results in lower energy 
consumption to produce concentrated products of the sa me quality.  The input and output 
electrolyte conductivities of our system (0.5 M K 2SO4: ~80 mS/cm; 10 wt% HCOOH: ~7.8 
mS/cm) were comparable to those of the reference (10% KCOOH: ~50 mS/cm; 30% HCOOH: 
~10.5 mS/cm).17,18 Moreover, since our system operated at a lower current density, the overall cell 
voltage should be comparable to or lower than that of the reference system. Consequently, the 
electricity consumption related to the OPEX  of electrodialysis was only adjusted based on the 
current efficiency:

membranes (EDBPM) is employed to reconvert potassium formate into formic acid, following the 
process demonstrated by Mahinder Ramdin12, as shown in Supplementary Fig. 51.
